The second identity gives \(\mathbb E[U^m B]=2(m+1)\mathbb E[U^m A^2]\), including \(m=0\) by taking a limit through positive \(m\). The function \((\sqrt v/2)\int_v^1\phi(u)u^{-3/2}\,du\) is bounded. Fubini shows that its \(m\)-th moment against the height law is \(\mathbb E[U^m B]/(2(m+1))\). Uniqueness against polynomial tests gives the conditional formula. As another consequence, the two algebraic roots \(A\pm i\sqrt{B-A^2}\) have zero averaged power sum at every positive order. This follows from the coefficients of the logarithm of the product of their linear factors. This power-sum identity involves two roots over each point and does not give a scalar regular Laurent phase.

### The icosahedral vertex and face arrangements

Let \(q=x_1^2+x_2^2+x_3^2\) and use \(p,h\) from (H4), with \(h=\nabla p\cdot\nabla p\). Put \(r=h+7q^2p\). Up to scale, \(r\) is the product of the ten hyperplanes normal to the face axes of the icosahedron whose vertex axes give \(p\). To check this, set \(a=x_1^2,b=x_2^2,c=x_3^2\), \(k=(3+\sqrt5)/2\), and \(k^{-1}=(3-\sqrt5)/2\). Then
\[
 r=\sqrt5\,\big(q^2-4(ab+bc+ca)\big)
          (kb-k^{-1}c)(ka-k^{-1}b)(k^{-1}a-kc).
\]
The first factor is the product of the four diagonal-axis hyperplanes. The other factors give the six face axes with one zero coordinate. For the standard vertices used in (H4), the directions \((1,1,1)\) and \((1/\varphi,0,\varphi)\), where \(\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2\), are face normals. Their signed cyclic permutations give the displayed list. Equality with \(h+7q^2p\) follows by expanding in the squared coordinates.

On \(q=1\) write \(V_p=\nabla p/(6p)\) and \(V_r=\nabla r/(10r)\). Their Gram entries satisfy
\[
 |V_p|^2=\frac{r-7p}{36p^2},\qquad
 V_p\cdot V_r=\frac{3r-5p(1+8p)}{60pr},\qquad
 |V_r|^2=\frac{(25+240p)r-15p(1+8p)^2}{100r^2}.
\]
These identities can be checked using \(\nabla f\cdot\nabla g
=4(a f_ag_a+b f_bg_b+c f_cg_c)\). In homogeneous form the two additional numerators before dividing by \(60pr\) and \(100r^2\) are \(3q^2r-5q^4p-40qp^2\) and \((25q^4+240qp)r-15p(q^3+8p)^2\).

For an explicit application take \(V=(V_p+V_r)/2\) and \(D=V_r-V_p\). The weights on the sixteen axes at the base point are all positive. If \(a_0,b_0,c_0\) are respectively the first, third, and middle Gram entries above, then the joint law in the theorem applies to
\[
                 U=\frac{4}{a_0+b_0+2c_0},\qquad
                 A=\frac{U}{2}(b_0-a_0),\qquad
                 B=U(a_0+b_0-2c_0).
\]
This is an all-order family of mixed identities in the icosahedral invariant function field. For example, any sufficiently small rational weight perturbation gives the same marginal height law while the first conditional directional moment is zero.

**Theorem (The field of the icosahedral arrangement data).**

Let \(v=|V_p|^{-2}\) and \(w=|V_r|^{-2}\) be the vertex and face heights. In characteristic zero the field \(K(p,h)\) has degree three over \(K(v,w)\). For an independent Laurent variable \(t\) one has
\[
                  K(t,v,w)\cap K[p,h,t,t^{-1}]=K[t,t^{-1}].
\]
The same intersection holds in the full rotation-invariant Laurent ring, since a function of \(v,w,t\) is also reflection invariant. Adjoining the mixed product \(c_0=V_p\cdot V_r\) to the two heights recovers the entire field \(K(p,h)\). Equivalently the three Gram entries above, or \(U,A,B\) in the weight formula, generate the full reflection-invariant field.

Indeed \(h=36p^2/v\), so \(r=p(7+36p/v)\) and
\[
 w=\frac{100p(7+36p/v)^2}
         {160+(1440+900/v)p+(8640/v-960)p^2}.
\]
As a rational function of \(p\) over \(K(v)\) it has degree three. The numerator roots are \(0,-7v/36\) with multiplicities one and two. The denominator at these points is \(160\) and \(-5(14v-9)^2/27\), respectively. Thus the rational map is reduced and has a finite pole in addition to the one at infinity. For every finite value of \(w\), the fibre has a finite point, by the cubic numerator and the absence of common factors. Consequently a nonconstant rational function of \(w\) over \(K(t,v)\) has a pole at a finite \(p\), whether it has a finite pole in \(w\) or a pole only at \(w=\infty\). On the other hand, substituting \(h=36p^2/v\) expresses a regular invariant as a polynomial in \(p\) over \(K(t,v)\). If this polynomial depends on \(v,t\) alone, the coefficient of \(p^0\) shows it is a Laurent scalar in \(t\). This gives the intersection.

The mixed entry is
\[
                c_0=\frac{16+(108/v-40)p}{60p(7+36p/v)}.
\]
It has simple poles at both points of the \(w=0\) fibre. A rational function of \(w\) with a pole there would have pole orders in ratio \(1:2\) at the two points, because those are the multiplicities of \(w\). Thus \(c_0\notin K(v,w)\). The extension degree is prime, so it generates \(K(v,p)=K(p,h)\) over the two-height field.

Unlike the octahedral pair, these two heights do not give the first two components of a Dirichlet split with remaining component \(1-v-w\). At \(x=(1,1,1)/\sqrt3\), the gradients of \(p,r\) are radial by cyclic symmetry, and both values are nonzero. Euler's identity gives \(v=w=1\) there and \(v+w>1\) on a neighborhood. The generic face-height fibre in the reflection-invariant plane is already a genus-one curve. Its quadratic equation in \(r\) has discriminant
\[
 w^2(25+240p)^2-6000wp(1+8p)^2 .
\]
As a cubic in \(p\) its discriminant is \(-276480000000000w^5(48w^2+33w+40)\), which is nonzero generically. Thus the face height alone cannot give a birational rational conditional parameter. The mixed Gram coordinate provides a full-field alternative for testing joint identities.

### Weight deformations of the rational witness

The scalar normalization admits a family of deformations. In any invariant arrangement with at least two distinct hyperplanes, let its weights be rational functions of \(t\), constant on group orbits, of sum one, and regular and strictly positive at \(t=0\). Form \(M(t),H(t)\) and \(U(t)\) as in (H1). With \(T=1+t\) the coefficientwise compact integral of
\[
                          \big[t^{-1}T(1-T^2U(t))^2\big]^m
\]
is the same formal Laurent series as for constant weights. Both the zero pure moment and the nonzero \(t\)-marked moment follow. Indeed the uniform bound on ratios of reciprocal sums applies also to these analytic weights near zero. Each deformed height has the same real law in an interval, so equality of the radial integrals there gives equality of their Taylor expansions.

The weight deformation still has a generic algebraic pole. Over an algebraic closure of \(K(t)\), each residue of the logarithmic gradient along its own hyperplane is nonzero, so \(\gcd(D,H(t))=1\). The polynomial \(H(t)\) vanishes at a nonisotropic point of the intersection of any two chosen real hyperplanes, where every summand of \(M(t)\) vanishes. Therefore it has a divisor outside the radial quadric. The finite étale passage from that complement to the unit sphere detects a pole of \(U(t)\) there. The phase above has that pole as well. Thus moving the weights together with the split variable preserves the moments formally and still requires a different denominator mechanism.


## Point-free forms for the compact rank-one group

Let \(C\) be the compact simply connected group of type \(A_1\) over a real closed field \(K\). In a standard compact maximal torus \(D\), write \(\mu_m\) for its cyclic subgroup of order \(m\). Choose a Weyl representative \(j\in N_C(D)(K)\) with \(j^2=-1\). The rank-one stabilizer list and the involution description of compact homogeneous forms give an explicit list of the point-free forms that still need a trace calculation.

**Theorem (Residual compact rank-one twists).**

Every point-free affine geometrically homogeneous \(C\)-space with an unresolved regular trace is of one of the following types, up to \(C\)-equivariant isomorphism:

| Geometric stabilizer | Involution class in the normalizer quotient |
| --- | --- |
| Trivial | The central involution, giving the norm-minus-one quaternion torsor |
| \(\mu_m\), \(m\ge3\) | The unique nontrivial toral involution modulo \(\mu_m\) |
| \(D\) | The Weyl involution, giving the negative unit sphere of the ternary adjoint norm |
| Binary dihedral of order \(4m\), \(m\ge3\) | The nontrivial class represented in \(D\) by a primitive \(4m\)-th root |
| \(Q_8\) or \(2T\) | The nontrivial involution class represented by a primitive eighth root in a suitable \(D\) |

The binary dihedral forms are finite equivariant quotients of the toral cyclic forms with stabilizer \(\mu_{2m}\). The \(Q_8\) and \(2T\) forms are finite equivariant quotients of the toral cyclic form with stabilizer \(\mu_4\). A toral cyclic form of order \(m=2^s k\) with \(k\) odd is a quotient of the toral cyclic form of order \(2^s\). In particular, whenever one of these target quotients has a witness, the indicated cyclic cover has one. Mathieu–Zhao for a source cover implies Mathieu–Zhao for the target.

Here is the stabilizer and cohomology calculation. Over \(K(i)\), connected proper reductive stabilizers in \(\mathrm{SL}_2\) have identity component a maximal torus, so are that torus or its normalizer. The finite possibilities are the cyclic, binary dihedral, tetrahedral, octahedral and icosahedral groups in [Flicker, Section 2](https://www.ariel.ac.il/wp/yuval-flicker/wp-content/uploads/sites/316/2023/06/94.FiniteSubgpsSL2Manusc.pdf). A compact reference representative of each geometric conjugacy class exists by the compact form argument above. On the quotient by a fixed reference stabilizer \(L\), point-free forms correspond to conjugacy classes of nonidentity involutions in \(N_C(L)/L\). This uses the compact cocycle classification and point criterion above. The case \(L=C\) is a point.

For \(L=1\), the sole nonidentity involution is \(-1\). For \(L=\mu_2\), the commuting group is compact adjoint of rank one, and a nontrivial involution gives an isotropic commuting twist. This gives a witness by the split rank-one obstruction. For \(L=\mu_m\), \(m\ge3\), its centralizer torus is \(D\), and its normalizer is \(D\cup Dj\). The connected component of the normalizer quotient is an anisotropic circle. There is one nontrivial toral involution. Every element of \(Dj\) squares to \(-1\). Thus Weyl involutions descend to the quotient precisely for even \(m\), and all are conjugate, since squaring on the torus points is surjective. Their commuting torus becomes split and the explicit cyclic witness above applies. In any toral case the chosen cyclic cover of order \(2^s\) has involution represented by a primitive \(2^{s+1}\)-th root. Its image modulo \(\mu_m\) is the nontrivial involution because \(k\) is odd. This includes \(s=0\) for odd \(m\), when the source is the norm-minus-one quaternion torsor.

For \(L=D\) the quotient \(C/D\) is the positive unit sphere in the adjoint representation. The right Weyl action sends the axis vector to its negative. Combining this action with compact conjugation descends the negative unit sphere, by multiplication by \(i\) in the ambient complex Lie algebra. For \(L=N_C(D)\) the stabilizer is self-normalizing, so there is only the pointed form.

A binary dihedral group of order \(4m\) is \(L=\langle\mu_{2m},j\rangle\). For \(m\ge3\) its characteristic rotation subgroup is generated by its elements of order greater than four. Any normalizer therefore preserves \(D\). For \(s\in D\), the condition \(sjs^{-1}=s^2j\in L\) is exactly \(s\in\mu_{4m}\). This yields the claimed index-two normalizer and representative. For \(Q_8\) and \(2T\) the normalizer is \(2O\), as in the no-point principal-stabilizer argument. The respective quotients are \(S_3\) and the group of order two. A lift \(n\) of a quarter-turn about a coordinate axis has order eight and \(n^2\in Q_8\). Its classes represent the required involutions, and all transpositions are conjugate in \(S_3\). The two larger polyhedral stabilizers are self-normalizing and introduce no point-free forms.

In each displayed map of twists the subgroup inclusion gives the usual geometric quotient map, and the descent representatives induce the same involution downstairs. This proves existence of the maps over \(K\). They preserve normalized traces by geometric transitivity, and pullback is injective on regular functions. In particular the transfer of a witness goes from target to source. The cyclic-cover reduction distinguishes the toral twist family from the Weyl twists already settled by the split commuting torus.


### The face-height differential has a non-torsion residue divisor

Use \(p,r,w\) from the vertex and face calculation. Write \(f(p,r)\) for the polynomial on the right of (H4) at \(q=1\) and \(h=r-7p\). The orientation coordinate can be normalized to satisfy \(J^2=f\). The face-height fibre on the reflection quotient and its orientation cover have equations
\[
 G_w(p,r)=100r^2-w(25+240p)r+15wp(1+8p)^2=0,\qquad J^2=f(p,r).
\]

**Theorem (The face-height conditional differential).**

The smooth projective orientation cover of the generic icosahedral face-height fibre has genus four. Its conditional differential, up to the height normalization, is
\[
                   \eta_w=\frac{r^2\,dp}{(\partial G_w/\partial r)J}.
\]
It has two simple poles with opposite nonzero residues. The difference of the two pole points has infinite order in the Jacobian. Consequently, even after finite algebraic covers or algebraic extensions of the height field, this conditional differential cannot be made logarithmic up to a scalar and a differential with zero residues.

For the differential, the area density on the angular invariant plane is proportional to \(dp\,dr/\sqrt f\), since \(r=h+7p\). Differentiating \(G_w\) gives \(\partial r/\partial w=100r^2/(w\partial G_w/\partial r)\). This identifies the oriented conditional differential, with the factor \(100/w\) absorbed into the height normalization.

Let \(E_w\) be the smooth projective reflection fibre. Its discriminant in \(r\) is cubic in \(p\) and squarefree generically, by the preceding computation. Thus \(E_w\) is elliptic with a single point \(O\) at infinity. There \(p,r\) have poles of orders two and three. The polynomial \(f\) has a pole of order ten, since its term \(8640p^5\) has strictly highest pole order. At finite points, \(f\) has two double zeros, at \(r=0\) with \(p=0,-1/8\), and six simple zeros. Here are exact ways to check the multiplicities and their persistence. The two indicated points are smooth with local parameters \(p\) and \(p+1/8\), respectively. The leading coefficients of \(f\) there are \(-64/25\) and \(-25/4\). At \(w=1/2\) the resultant in \(r\) of \(f,G_w\) is
\[
 5000p^2(8p+1)^2
    \big(233280000p^6+119880000p^5+21261800p^4+1456955p^3
                  +26342p^2-277p-7\big).
\]
The sextic is squarefree and disjoint from the cubic discriminant and the two double zeros. This can be checked by the same gcd tests modulo each of \(11,13,17,19\). Indeed, on the smooth elliptic model the two double zeros \(P_0,P_1\) and the infinity \(O\) are disjoint sections. The divisor of \(f\) is \(2P_0+2P_1-10O\) plus the six residual zeros. Regard \(f\) as a section of \(\mathcal O(5O-P_0-P_1)^{\otimes2}\), so the double cover branches only over that residual divisor. The gcd checks give six distinct branch points in each reduction, and in odd characteristic the double cover of this smooth model is smooth. This gives good reduction at all four primes. Over a neighborhood of \(w=1/2\) the two double zeros remain sections with the same multiplicity, the other branch points remain simple and disjoint, and the degree and leading coefficient at infinity remain nonzero. Thus the normalization is smooth in that neighborhood. Riemann–Hurwitz for the double cover with six branch points gives genus four.

The pullback of \(dp/(\partial G_w/\partial r)\) is holomorphic. At a simple zero of \(f\) it has a zero of order one from the ramification, which cancels the zero of \(J\). At the double zeros of \(f\), \(r^2\) vanishes to at least order two while \(J\) has order one. At the two points \(I_\pm\) of the orientation cover above \(O\), the ratio \(r^2/J\) has a simple pole. The involution \(J\mapsto-J\) exchanges the two poles and negates the differential. This proves the residue assertion.

It remains to rule out torsion. Specialize \(w=1/2\). At \(O\), set \(Y=\partial G_{1/2}/\partial r\), so \(Y^2\) has leading coefficient \(-192000\) as a cubic in \(p\). With local parameter \(p/Y\), the leading coefficient of \(p\) is \(-1/192000\). The square class of the leading coefficient of \(f\) is therefore that of \(8640(-192000)\), namely \(-2\). Thus \(I_\pm\) are defined over \(\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-2})\).

The [exact replay checker](verify_icosahedral_face_height.py) computes point counts of the normalized genus-four curve at the four good primes. In each finite field it enumerates \(p\), solves the quadratic \(G_{1/2}\) for \(r\), and counts square roots of \(f\). At the two double zeros it instead counts square roots of their leading coefficients, which gives the points of the normalization. It counts the two points over infinity by the square class \(-2\). The point counts determine the degree-eight numerator \(L_\ell(T)\) of the zeta function via Newton's identities and the functional equation. The Jacobian point counts are \(L_\ell(1)\) over \(\mathbb F_\ell\) and \(L_\ell(1)L_\ell(-1)\) over \(\mathbb F_{\ell^2}\) by [Weil's formula, as stated in Milne, Section 11](https://jmilne.org/math/xnotes/JVs.pdf).

| Prime \(\ell\) | Counts over degrees one through four | \(L_\ell(1)\) | \(L_\ell(-1)\) if the pole points are nonsplit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11 | \(20,156,1304,14544\) | \(34580\) | |
| 13 | \(12,208,2466,28704\) | \(28512\) | \(35904\) |
| 17 | \(20,326,4814,84506\) | \(99600\) | |
| 19 | \(20,414,6518,130254\) | \(137775\) | |

Over a field of good reduction of residue characteristic \(\ell\), prime-to-\(\ell\) torsion injects under reduction. Indeed, in the formal group, multiplication by an integer prime to \(\ell\) has invertible linear term and cannot kill a nonzero point in the reduction kernel. Apply this at these primes to the difference \(I_+-I_-\) over \(\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-2})\). At 13 use the residue field of degree two, and at the other primes use degree one. The gcd of the four applicable group sizes is one. Each of the four residue characteristics also fails to divide at least one size at a different prime. Thus a torsion difference would have order one. That is impossible for distinct points on a curve of positive genus, since a function with one simple pole gives a degree-one map to the projective line. Smooth specialization from the generic fibre implies infinite order there too.

For any finite cover, push a putative residue relation back down to the face-height curve. The residue divisor of a logarithmic differential is principal, that of a residue-free differential is zero, and pushforward after pullback multiplies divisor classes by the cover degree. The non-torsion class therefore contradicts such a relation. This closes the logarithmic transport route for the face arrangement as well as for the vertex arrangement.


For independent vertex and face heights the pullbacks of the two conditional differentials to any common finite covering curve are also independent modulo residue-free and logarithmic differentials. The vertex Jacobian is an elliptic curve with nonconstant \(j(v)\) by the vertex calculation. Over the algebraic closure of the field of \(w\), the face Jacobian has only finitely many elliptic simple factors up to isogeny. Passing to a larger algebraically closed field creates no additional simple factors. One way to see the latter fact is invariance of the homomorphism group of abelian varieties under algebraically closed extension: spread a homomorphism over a connected finite-type base and apply the rigidity lemma after subtracting one specialized homomorphism. Any isogeny to one of the elliptic factors would make \(j(v)\) algebraic over the field of \(w\), since the finite kernel of an isogeny from a fixed elliptic curve is defined over that algebraic closure. Thus the two Jacobians admit no homomorphisms between them. Taking residues and pushing forward separately to the vertex and face curves gives the claimed independence, using the infinite-order divisor on each.


## A logarithmic conditional coordinate for the icosahedral mirrors

The vertex-axis and face-axis heights give conditional differentials with non-torsion residue divisors. The arrangement consisting of the fifteen actual reflection mirrors has a different conditional law. Its conditional circle can be written explicitly and retains an additional two-valued variable on the angular quotient.

Let \(p,h,r\) be the icosahedral invariants above, with \(r=h+7q^2p\). Let \(J\) be one fourth of the Jacobian of \(q,p,h\), so \(J^2=f\) in (H4). Up to scalar, \(J\) is the product of the mirror forms. In fact it is anti-invariant, vanishes on all fifteen mirrors, and has degree fifteen. On \(q=1\) set
\[
 a=45p^2+13p+1-5r,\qquad b=19p^2+3p+(15p-1)r,\qquad
 H=\nabla J\cdot\nabla J .
\]
The Gram formulas give \(H=-80ab\) on \(q=1\). Before setting \(q=1\) the factors are \(45p^2+13pq^3+q^6-5qr\) and \(19p^2q^2+3pq^5+(15p-q^3)r\). For a check, compute \((\nabla f\cdot\nabla f)/(4f)\) in the coordinates \(q,p,r\). The additional products with \(\nabla q\) are \(4q,12p,20r\), and the other three Gram entries are the ones above. The mirror height is \(w=225f/H\) on \(q=1\). It is the law (H1) for the fifteen mirror forms with equal weights.

**Theorem (The icosahedral mirror conditional circle).**

Use normalized area on the sphere. For \(0<w<1\), put \(\alpha=3\sqrt5\), \(d=w^2-22w+45\) and define
\[
 \xi=\frac{\alpha J}{4\sqrt w\,a},\qquad X=\xi^2=-\frac b a,\qquad
 Y=\frac{24p-(w-3)X}{\xi},\qquad
 R=\frac{(9-w)\sqrt{(25-w)(1-w)}}{2\alpha}.
\]
Then \((\alpha X-d/(2\alpha),Y)=R(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\). The three variables \(\sqrt w,\theta,\operatorname{sgn}\xi\) are independent under area measure. The first is uniform on \([0,1]\), the second uniform on the circle, and the third uniform on the two signs. The original invariants can be reconstructed by
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 X&=\frac{d}{90}+\frac R\alpha\cos\theta,\qquad
 \xi=\operatorname{sgn}(\xi)\sqrt X,\qquad Y=R\sin\theta,\\
 p&=\frac{(w-3)X+\xi Y}{24},\\
 r&=3p(1-X)-X^2+X-\frac{16}{45}wX,\qquad
 J=\frac{4\sqrt w}{\alpha}a\xi .
 \end{aligned}
\]
In particular the conditional differential on the orientation curve is logarithmic after an algebraic extension of the height field. The curve has genus one, and the circle map has degree two.

To prove the formulas, use \(U=b/a\) and \(Z=5U+15p-1\), so \(a=(5p+1)^2(27p-1)/Z\). On \(q=1\), the mirror fibre is \(G=225f+80wab=0\). Substitution gives the identity
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 G&=\frac{5a^2}{Z} \mathcal P(p,U;w),\\
 \mathcal P(p,U;w)
    &=2880p^2+240U(w-3)p-225U^3+(80w-180)U^2-16wU .
 \end{aligned}
\]
Thus \((24p+(w-3)U)^2=U(45U^2+dU+16w/5)\). Substitute \(U=-\xi^2\) to obtain
\[
                          Y^2=-45\xi^4+d\xi^2-\frac{16}{5}w.
\]
Together with \(d^2-576w=(w-25)(w-9)^2(w-1)\), this proves the circle equation. Reducing the expression \(r=(45p^2+13p+1-a)/5\) with \(\mathcal P=0\) gives the stated reconstruction. The even quartic has four distinct roots at a generic height, and its orientation-curve function field is recovered by these formulas. The quotient by \((\xi,Y)\mapsto(-\xi,Y)\) is the circle conic.

For the real support, \(w\) extends continuously by zero on the mirrors, by the reciprocal-gradient formula and positivity of the mirror weights. It reaches one at a maximum of \(|J|\), where the log gradient is radial. Connectedness gives each intermediate height. The level set of any \(w\in(0,1)\) is compact and avoids the mirrors. There \(J,H,a,b\) are all nonzero. The equation for \(Y^2\) shows that \(X\) ranges between two positive endpoints in the circle parametrization. On each sign of \(\xi\), the displayed reconstruction is analytic in \(w,\theta\). Since \((p,r)\) is a local coordinate pair away from the mirrors, this analytic left inverse makes \((w,\theta)\) local coordinates there. The image at a fixed height and sign is therefore open in the circle and compact. Both signs occur because inversion reverses \(J\) and fixes \(w,a\). Thus the whole circle is attained for each sign.

The area density in the two invariants is proportional to \(|dp\,dr/J|\). To change variables to the mirror fibre, differentiate \(G=0\) and use \(H/J=(225/w)J\). On the fibre \(G_r=5a\mathcal P_U\), since \(U_r=Z/a\). Also \(\mathcal P_p=240\xi Y\). Consequently
\[
 \frac{J}{G_r}\,dp
       =-\frac{J}{5a\mathcal P_p}\,dU
       =\frac{\sqrt w}{300\alpha}\frac{dX}{Y}.
\]
The last factor satisfies \(dX/Y=-d\theta/\alpha\). These formulas hold off their coordinate singularities and extend as differentials. The resulting area density is proportional to \(w^{-1/2}dw\,d\theta\), the same for both signs. Any multiplicity in the original sphere-to-invariant map is constant on the regular quotient: the map separates rotation orbits and the group acts freely away from the mirrors. This proves the stated product law. On the algebraic curve the function \(\alpha X-d/(2\alpha)+iY\) has logarithmic derivative \(-i\alpha\,dX/Y\). Its product with \(\alpha X-d/(2\alpha)-iY\) is \(R^2\), and it has a double zero and a double pole at the two points at infinity. This also explains the contrast with the vertex and face fibres.

### Regularity requires the two-valued variable

The circle alone, even with both sine and cosine and arbitrary rational dependence on the split variable, has a strict regularity obstruction.

**Theorem (Regular intersection for the mirror circle).**

Let \(K\) have characteristic zero, and allow an algebraic extension of the base field \(K(w)\) to form the circle coordinates. If a rational function of \(t,w,\cos\theta,\sin\theta\) is a regular element of \(K[p,r,J][t,t^{-1}]\), it is a Laurent scalar in \(t\). The result applies to the full angular rotation quotient, including orientation-odd functions. It also applies to a rational function defined by this chart on the compact group quotient if it is regular there, since its left-circle invariance forces descent to the angular ring.

For the proof let \(\kappa\) be the involution fixing \(w,X,Y\) and sending \(\xi\) to \(-\xi\). It acts on the original rational function field, since
\[
 p'=\kappa(p)=\frac{(w-3)X}{12}-p,\quad
 r'=\kappa(r)=3p'(1-X)-X^2+X-\frac{16}{45}wX,\quad
 J'=\kappa(J)=-J\,a'/a,
 \qquad a'=a(p',r').
\]
The map is unchanged by scalar extension in \(w\), and every rational circle expression in the statement is fixed by \(\kappa\). Its action commutes with inversion \(J\mapsto-J\).

Use the divisor \(a=0\), with old \(p\) a free residual parameter. Put \(c=(5p+1)^2(27p-1)\). For the calculation substitute \(r=(45p^2+13p+1-a)/5\). Then
\[
 b=\frac{(1-15p)a+c}{5},\qquad
 f=\frac{a^3}{5}-\frac{(675p^2-130p+11)a^2}{25}
                 +\frac{c(45p-7)a}{25}-\frac{c^2}{25}.
\]
One has \(X=-b/a\) and \(wX=45f/(16a^2)\). The leading coefficients in the expansions there are
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 a^2p'&\longrightarrow -\frac{3c^2}{320},\\
 a^3r'&\longrightarrow -\frac{9c^3}{1600},\\
 a^5\bigl(5r'^2+192p'^3\bigr)
       &\longrightarrow \frac{27c^5(45p+31)}{512000}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
They follow by substituting the two displayed expressions for \(b,f\) in \(p',r'\), with the leading terms of order six cancelling in \(5r'^2+192p'^3\).

Every polynomial in \(p,r\) has a unique expression in monomials
\[
                  p^i r^\epsilon (5r^2+192p^3)^j,\qquad \epsilon\in\{0,1\},
\]
by division by the monic relation quadratic in \(r\). After applying \(\kappa\), a monomial has pole order \(2i+3\epsilon+5j\). For a fixed highest order the leading terms are nonzero constant multiples of the same power of \(c\) times distinct powers of \(45p+31\). For each \(j\), the parity determines \(\epsilon\), then the order determines \(i\). Thus the coefficients cannot cancel, even over \(K(t)\). Every nonconstant polynomial in \(p,r\) acquires a pole at \(a=0\). On each component of this divisor in the orientation cover, \(J\) is a unit, since \(f|_{a=0}=-c^2/25\). Also \(a'\) has pole order four, so \(J'=-J a'/a\) has pole order five. A nonzero expression \(J\) times a polynomial in \(p,r\) therefore acquires a pole as well. Separate an invariant Laurent polynomial into its even and odd parts under inversion, which are independently invariant. Only the Laurent scalars in the even part survive.

This identifies the role of the extra sign in the conditional law. For example a joint endpoint calculation with only the transported mirror height and the full conditional circle can produce rational pure phases but has no angular-dependent regular member. A regular construction must use the two-valued coordinate, or a different joint identity.


In fact a regular function is also excluded if it is anti-invariant under \(\kappa\). For every nonconstant angular regular phase \(P\), the two-sheet trace \(P^m+\kappa(P)^m\) has a genuine pole at \(a=0\) for every \(m\ge1\). This follows from the same pole calculation, separating the even and odd parts. Alternatively, over the two components of \(a=0\) in the orientation cover, cancellation between those two parts at highest pole order on both components would require both leading coefficients to vanish. The sheet sign therefore cannot simply be averaged out before regularization.

### Polynomial mirror moments and a prime obstruction

The mirror preview of the angular probability law gives a separate arithmetic test. It applies even when the leading angular coefficient is a power of the discriminant, which has two leading monomials in these coordinates.

**Theorem (A mirror-height prime obstruction).**

Use weights \(3,5,15/2\) for \(p,r,J\), and let \(f=J^2\). Let \(P\) be a regular Laurent phase on the icosahedral angular quotient, over a characteristic-zero field. Suppose its highest angular-weight part equals
\[
                 \gamma\,p^i r^j f^k t^{-c}(1+t)^b,\qquad
 D=3i+5j+15k>0,\qquad 1\le c\le b\le 2D ,
\]
where \(\gamma\ne0\). Then every nonconstant polynomial in \(P\) has infinitely many nonzero pure moments. In particular, for every \(N\ge1\) the regular cleared phase
\[
                     \frac{1+t}{t}\bigl(H-225(1+t)^2 f\bigr)^N
\]
and all its nonconstant polynomial transforms fail eventual pure vanishing.

To prove this, the conditional formula above makes every polynomial conditional moment a polynomial in \(w\) with coefficients integral away from \(2,3,5\) for integral input. Expand in \(X,Y,\xi\), using the two signs of \(\xi\) to remove the odd powers. The remaining powers of \(\xi\) are powers of \(X\), and the relevant powers of \(Y=R\sin\theta\) are even. Odd powers of \(\cos\theta\) integrate to zero. This leaves only \(R^2\), with the rational circle moments which have denominators powers of two. Odd powers of \(J\) have conditional trace zero by inversion, and even ones are powers of \(f\). The formulas give conditional degree at most angular weight. In detail \(X,Y\) have degree at most two in the formal expansion at \(w=\infty\), \(\xi\) has degree at most one, and the formulas for \(p,r,f\) give degrees at most \(3,5,15\).

The leading law is especially simple. Formally take \(w\) large positive and divide \(p,r,f\) by \(w^3,w^5,w^{15}\), respectively. At such \(w\), choose the positive radius in the same formulas, which leaves the polynomial conditional traces unchanged. With \(u\) of arcsine law on \([-1,1]\), the limiting variables are
\[
 P_\infty=\frac{(1-u)(1+u)^2}{1080},\qquad
 R_\infty=-\frac{(1-u)^2(1+u)^3}{16200},\qquad
 f_\infty=16P_\infty^4(1-u^2).
\]
Indeed \(X/w^2\) tends to \(\zeta/45\), where \(\zeta=(1+\cos\theta)/2\), and \((\xi Y)/w^3\) tends to \(\zeta\operatorname{sgn}(\xi\sin\theta)\sqrt{1-\zeta}/45\). The variable \(u=\operatorname{sgn}(\xi\sin\theta)\sqrt{1-\zeta}\) is arcsine on \([-1,1]\). Use the formulas for \(p,r,J\) to get the limits. These are limits of the coefficients of the conditional laws, without a real fibre at \(w>1\). A related sharpness fact is that for a nonzero real angular polynomial with weight \(d\), its positive even-power conditional moments have exactly the maximal degree allowed by this weight. For \(p^i r^j\) of a fixed weight \(3i+5j\), the distinct exponents \(i+2j\) of \(1-u\) give linearly independent limiting terms. An orientation-odd leading part is a polynomial times \(J\), whose normalized square has a positive nonzero limit off the endpoints. The leading even powers therefore have positive limiting integral.

For the top monomial in the theorem, the limiting integral of its \(m\)-th power is a nonzero rational scalar power times the arcsine moment of \((1-u)^{am}(1+u)^{em}\), where \(a=i+2j+5k\) and \(e=2i+3j+9k\). Thus \(a+e=D-k\le D\), and that integral is a unit at every sufficiently large prime \(\ell=2Dm+1\). This follows directly from the beta factors \((1/2)_{am}(1/2)_{em}/((a+e)m)!\), with the powers of two harmless. The top Laurent constant term contributes \(\binom{bm}{cm}\), likewise a unit. Integrating a polynomial in \(w\) against the squared-uniform law divides its coefficient of \(w^d\) by \(2d+1\). Since all smaller angular weights yield conditional degrees below \(Dm\), only the top coefficient supplies a pole at \(\ell\). The trace therefore has valuation exactly minus one. For a polynomial of degree \(s>0\) in \(P\), use primes \(2Dsm+1\); only its highest power can supply this pole. Coefficients in an arbitrary characteristic-zero field can first be specialized to a number field, retaining the nonzero leading coefficients and the specified highest angular part. Infinitely many primes of the desired form follow from the cyclotomic argument used for the previous prime test. For the cleared phase, \(H=-80ab\) has weight fourteen and \(f\) weight fifteen. Thus its entire top part is a scalar times \(f^N t^{-1}(1+t)^{2N+1}\), as required.

The new circle law leaves a mixed-sheet route. In that chart each phase decomposes over the circle field as \(F_0+\xi F_1\), and conditional sign averaging gives the exact sum \(\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\binom m{2j}F_0^{m-2j}X^jF_1^{2j}\). The pure fixed-circle endpoint identity does not settle the joint cancellation of these terms with the remaining height integral.


## A flat angular reduction for the norm-minus-one quaternion torsor

Let \(K\) be real closed and let \(X_-\) be the torsor of quaternions \(x\) of norm \(-1\). The odd quaternion power maps \(x\mapsto x^k\) are regular self-maps of \(X_-\).

**Theorem (Odd-power pullback and a transverse prime test).**

For every odd \(k\ge3\), pullback along \(x\mapsto x^k\) gives the same functional \(\lambda_{\rm flat}\) on regular functions. On the compact reference sphere, this functional is the law of
\[
           (x_0,x_1,x_2,x_3)=i(\cos\theta,\sin\theta\, n_1,\sin\theta\, n_2,\sin\theta\, n_3),
\]
where \(\theta\) is uniform on the circle and \(n\) is independently uniform on the unit two-sphere. Thus a phase and fixed marker for this flat trace give a regular witness on \(X_-\) by the same odd-power pullback.

In these flat coordinates, let \(F(a,x)\in K[a,x]\), where \(a=x_0\) and \(x=x_1\) are the indicated coordinate functions of \(X_-\). Let \(d=\deg F\) and \(R=\deg_x F\ge1\), with \(d<2R\), and write \(A(a)=[x^R]F\). If \(A(i)\ne0\), then \(F\) has infinitely many nonzero positive moments for \(\lambda_{\rm flat}\). In particular the pullback phase has infinitely many nonzero normalized moments on \(X_-\). This includes every bivariate phase whose highest homogeneous part has a nonzero \(x^d\) coefficient.

For the flat law, over the complex numbers identify the torsor with the norm-one group by \(x=i g\). In quaternion polar coordinates \(g=\cos\theta+n\sin\theta\), Haar measure is the pushforward of \((1-\cos(2\theta))d\theta/(2\pi)\) times uniform \(n\) on the two-sphere. This follows from the Jacobian \(\sin^2\theta\) on the unit three-sphere. The power map multiplies \(\theta\) by \(k\), up to the central sign \(i^{k-1}\) when passing back to \(X_-\). For \(k\ge3\) no nonzero harmonic of the density is divisible by \(k\), so the angle after the map is uniform. The central sign preserves this law. The resulting polynomial moments are rational, computed by the sphere and circle moment formulas, so the pullback identities hold over \(K\) by base change.

For the prime test, \(x_1=i \sin\theta\,s\) with \(s\) uniform on \([-1,1]\) independent of \(\theta\). Consequently the flat trace of \(a^u x^v\) is zero if either exponent is odd, and otherwise
\[
   \lambda_{\rm flat}(a^{2i}x^{2j})
        =\frac{(-1)^{i+j}}{2j+1}\frac{(1/2)_i(1/2)_j}{(i+j)!}.
\]
Specialize the coefficient ring of \(F\) to a number field, keeping \(A(i),A(-i)\) nonzero. Both are nonzero before specialization because the coefficients lie in a real closed field. Adjoin \(i\) and an \(R\)-th root of \(A(i)/A(-i)\), and choose arbitrarily large degree-one split primes \(\ell\) of that field, with \(\ell\equiv1\bmod 2R\) and avoiding all fixed coefficient denominators and zeros. One can use primes split completely in a normal closure after adjoining the required roots of unity. Put \(m=(\ell-1)/R\). Then \(m\) is even. In \(F^m\), every even monomial \(a^{2i}x^{2j}\) has \(i+j\le dm/2<\ell-1\); all factorial denominators in the displayed moment are units. Only \(j=(\ell-1)/2\) can have a pole from the factor \(2j+1\), and its entire coefficient polynomial is \(A(a)^m\).

For \(j=(\ell-1)/2\) one has \((j+1)_i\equiv(1/2)_i\pmod\ell\), with all factors units because \(i+j<\ell-1\). Multiplying the trace by \(\ell\) and reducing, its residue is therefore a fixed unit times
\[
                          \frac{A(i)^m+A(-i)^m}{2}.
\]
The \(R\)-th root of their ratio belongs to the prime field at these primes, hence the ratio to the power \(m\) is one there. The residue is nonzero. Its nonzero orders in the specialization imply the same nonvanishing over the original field. This tests bivariate candidates in the flat model even when their quaternion power pullbacks have arbitrarily large ambient degree. It leaves in particular candidates whose transverse top coefficient is divisible by \(a^2+1\), and phases with more spatial coordinates.

### A limit on universal realification by a Laurent variable

A construction that uses only the complex pure moments by means of a pointwise harmonic identity has a basic limitation. This applies to attempts to realify the complex exceptional witnesses without using their further mixed moments.

**Theorem (Pointwise harmonic realification forces one Laurent sign).**

Let \(F(A,B,t)\in\mathbb R[A,B,t,t^{-1}]\). Suppose that for every positive \(m\), the polynomial \(\operatorname{CT}_t F^m\) is a linear combination of the positive powers of \(A+iB\) and \(A-iB\). Then \(F\) has all its nonzero \(t\)-exponents strictly positive, or all strictly negative. In particular every such construction produces only safe phases after substituting any \(A,B\) in a coefficient algebra with trace.

Substitute \(A=(\rho/2)(z+z^{-1})\) and \(B=(\rho/(2i))(z-z^{-1})\) for a real \(\rho>0\). The hypothesis makes every positive power of the resulting Laurent polynomial have zero joint constant term in \(z,t\). Choose \(\rho\) so each nonzero \(t\)-coefficient in \(F\) remains a nonzero trigonometric polynomial. This is possible since otherwise a nonzero real polynomial would vanish on every circle in an open annulus. For every exponent pair \((h,j)\) of \(z,t\) the pair \((-h,j)\) also occurs, by reality of the coefficient functions. The convex hull therefore contains \((0,j)\) for each retained \(t\)-exponent \(j\). If those \(j\)'s straddle or include zero, the convex hull contains the origin, contradicting the Laurent theorem of [Duistermaat and van der Kallen](https://webspace.science.uu.nl/~kalle101/powers.pdf). If \(F=0\), either one-sided statement is vacuous. Otherwise a strict sign in \(t\) makes every fixed Laurent multiplier eventually miss constant weight. A successful realification using both Laurent signs must use joint integrals beyond the pointwise harmonic identities of the complex phase.


### Regular cancellation on the fixed mirror interval

The circle with its two sheets can give fixed-interval cancellation even for a regular phase with both Laurent signs. The moving endpoint is a separate contribution.

**Theorem (A regular phase with fixed-interval cancellation).**

Put \(T=1+t\) and \(P=t^{-15}T^{16}J\) on the icosahedral compact product. In the mirror chart make the height substitution \(\sqrt W=T\sqrt w\), keeping \(\theta\) fixed. Replace the new interval \([0,T]\) for \(\sqrt W\) by \([0,1]\). In this fixed-interval term, the scalar constant term of \(P^m\) vanishes for every \(m\ge1\) after averaging over both signs of \(\xi\) and both signs of the conditional radius. The second sign average leaves the integrated angle trace unchanged. The original phase is not pure: its moments of all positive even orders are nonzero.

For the fixed-interval assertion put \(y=T/t\). Then \(dt/(tT)=-dy/y\). At fixed generic \(W,\theta\) work on the finite covering curve of the \(y\)-line obtained by adjoining the conditional radius \(\sqrt{d(w)^2-576w}\) and \(\xi=\sqrt X\), with \(w=W/T^2\). The product law uses the average over the two \(\xi\) signs; averaging over the other sign is the shift \(\theta\mapsto\theta+\pi\), so we can use the trace over the full cover. The function \(\sqrt w=\sqrt W/T\) is already a rational scalar in this change of variables. The formulas for \(X,Y,p,r,J\) exhibit \(P\) as regular above every finite \(y\) other than potentially \(0\) or \(1\). At \(y=0\), \(T=0\) and \(w\) has pole order two in \(T\). The mirror weights give pole orders at most six, ten and fifteen in \(T\) for \(p,r,J\), respectively. This makes \(T^{16}J\) vanish on every branch there. At \(y=1\), \(T\) has a pole and \(w\) tends to zero. The chart gives finite \(p,r,\xi,Y\) and \(J=O(T^{-1})\), so \(t^{-15}T^{16}J\) is regular. These bounds hold with orders multiplied by the ramification index at any ramified point. Thus \(P^m\,dy/y\) has all its residues over \(y=\infty\). Their sum is zero by the residue theorem. These are precisely the scalar constant terms used by the fixed-interval calculation, with normalized averaging over the covering branches.

In the original variables the trace at even \(m\) is \(\binom{16m}{15m}\lambda(J^m)>0\) over the compact real model. Thus the entire contribution at those orders comes from the moving endpoint. This provides an explicit regular model for the remaining cancellation problem: a sheet-dependent construction can already cancel the bulk integral, and must also arrange the moving-endpoint term.

A projective height change fixes both endpoints, but changes the residue calculation. Write \(s=\sqrt w\) and \(s'=Ts/\sqrt{1+(T^2-1)s^2}\), where \(T=1+t\). For \(T>0\) near one this takes \([0,1]\) onto itself. Put \(W=(s')^2\) and \(L=W+(1-W)T^2\). Then \(w=W/L\) and \(ds=T^2 L^{-3/2}ds'\). For any regular Laurent phase from the angular ring, its conditional \(m\)-th trace \(A_m(t,w)\) is polynomial in \(w\). At fixed \(W\) the scalar differential becomes
\[
              A_m(t,W/L)\frac{T^2\,dt}{t\,\nu^3},\qquad \nu^2=L .
\]
The differential is anti-invariant under \(\nu\mapsto-\nu\), so the residues at the fixed branch points \(L=0\) vanish. At \(t=0\) the lifts have \(\nu=1,-1\), and their residues are opposite. Consequently the residue sum on the full cover supplies no vanishing of the individual residue at \(\nu=1\), which is the scalar constant term for the integral. Fixing the interval in this way trades the moving endpoint for a sheet selection in the scalar step.

## Regular witnesses from a split plane over the second factor

**Theorem (A relative split-plane witness on products).**

Let \(K\) have characteristic zero, let \(X=\{q(x)=c\}\), where \(q\) is nondegenerate of dimension \(n\ge3\) and \(c\in K^\times\), and let \(Y\) be affine geometrically integral and homogeneous under a connected reductive group. Use the normalized \(\mathrm{SO}(q)\)-trace on \(X\) and the given normalized invariant trace on \(Y\). Suppose \(e_1,e_2\) are regular vector-valued functions of \(y\in Y\) with
\[
 q(e_1)=R,\qquad q(e_2)=-R,\qquad B(e_1,e_2)=0,\qquad R\ne0,
\]
where \(B\) is the bilinear form normalized by \(B(x,x)=q(x)\), and \(R\ne0\) means nonzero as a function. Put \(U=B(e_1,x)+B(e_2,x)\), \(V=(B(e_1,x)-B(e_2,x))/c\), and define the regular functions
\[
 P=(1+U)\big((1+U)^2 V-(2+U)R\big),\qquad
 F=P^{n-2},\qquad Q=U^{n-2}.
\]
For every \(m\ge1\), with \(k=(n-2)m\), the product trace satisfies
\[
 \Lambda_{X\times Y}(F^m)=0,\qquad
 \Lambda_{X\times Y}(QF^m)
    =\frac{k!}{(n/2)_k}\Lambda_Y(R^k).                       \tag{R1}
\]
In particular, nonzero moments of \(R^k\) at infinitely many such \(k\) give a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample. An isotropic \(q\) over \(K(Y)\) always supplies the required vectors, with \(R=M^2\) for a nonzero \(M\in K[Y]\).

For the calculation first work over \(K(Y)\). The plane spanned by \(e_1,e_2\) is nondegenerate and split. Its split rotation torus in \(\mathrm{SO}(q)\) gives \(U\) and \(V\) opposite weights. Write \(D=UV/R\). This is the squared length of the projection onto the plane divided by \(c\). Over an algebraic closure an orthogonal identification with the standard positive sphere computes all its moments by scalar extension. In that model \(D\) is the sum of the first two squared coordinates on the unit sphere, and \(s=\sqrt{1-D}\) has density \((n-2)s^{n-3}\) on \([0,1]\), by the Dirichlet law. The moment coefficients are rational and therefore hold over \(K(Y)\).

The formula for \(P\) is the regular polynomial obtained from \(R U^{-1}(1+U)(1-(1+U)^2(1-D))\). In the localization by \(U\), the unique weight-zero coefficient of \(U^\ell P^k\) is the \(t^0\)-coefficient of
\[
 R^k t^{\ell-k}(1+t)^k(1-(1+t)^2(1-D))^k .                 \tag{R2}
\]
Indeed \(D\) has weight zero and \(U\) nonzero weight. Since \(U^\ell P^k\) is regular for the two choices \(\ell=0,n-2\), its weight-zero component agrees with this regular polynomial in \(D\). Thus the calculation uses the normalized trace only on regular functions. Taking its trace in the quadric variable and substituting \(z=(1+t)s\) gives the scalar expression
\[
 R^k t^{\ell-k}(1+t)^{k-(n-2)}
        (n-2)\int_0^{1+t} z^{n-3}(1-z^2)^k\,dz .
\]
The integral is a polynomial in \(1+t\). Its difference from the value at \(1\) vanishes to order \(k+1\) at \(t=0\). For \(k\ge n-2\), the coefficient of \(t^k\) in \((1+t)^{k-(n-2)}\) is zero, and that of \(t^{k-(n-2)}\) is one. The integral at \(1\), including the factor \(n-2\), equals \(k!/(n/2)_k\). These are identities in \(K(Y)\). The partial trace in \(x\) of the two original regular functions belongs to \(K[Y]\), which injects into \(K(Y)\). Taking the remaining trace gives (R1).

If \(q\) is isotropic over \(K(Y)\), choose a hyperbolic pair \(v,w\) there, with \(q(v)=q(w)=0\) and \(B(v,w)=1/2\). Then \(v+w,v-w\) have norms \(1,-1\) and are orthogonal. Clearing their common denominators by \(M\ne0\) produces the required regular vectors. Conversely the existence of the stated vectors makes \(e_1+e_2\) a nonzero isotropic vector over \(K(Y)\).

### Negative norm levels and the level of a field

Write \(S_d^\epsilon=\{z_1^2+\cdots+z_d^2=\epsilon\}\), where \(d\ge2\) and \(\epsilon=\pm1\). The negative level is point-free over a real closed field, and is an affine homogeneous space for the definite orthogonal group. Both levels and their normalized traces have a rational model.

**Theorem (Regular witnesses on products of twisted norm quadrics).**

Let \(d\ge2\), let \(2^a\le d<2^{a+1}\), and let \(n>2^a\). For either \(\epsilon\), the normalized trace on \(S_n^\epsilon\times S_d^{-}\) has a regular counterexample with a fixed multiplier and a nonzero marked moment at every positive order over every characteristic-zero field. In particular, the product of two negative levels in dimensions at least two has such a witness unless both dimensions equal the same power of two. Over a formally real field, in that exceptional equal-dimension case the first quadratic form remains anisotropic over the function field of the second level, so the relative split-plane construction cannot decide it.

To prove the existence claim it suffices to work over \(\mathbb Q\). The level of \(\mathbb Q(S_d^-)\), meaning the least number of squares representing \(-1\), is at most \(d\) by the defining equation. It is a power of two by Pfister's theorem, hence at most \(2^a\). Thus the sum of \(n\) squares is isotropic there. Use the hyperbolic pair in the relative construction and choose the common denominator in the form \(M(y)=L(y)L(-y)\), where \(L\) first clears both vectors. This remains a valid common denominator. On the compact reference sphere \(y=i b\), \(\sum b_j^2=1\), the value \(M(i b)=|L(i b)|^2\) is real nonnegative. It is nonzero on an open set because the compact reference sphere is Zariski dense. Therefore every trace of \(R^k=M^{2k}\) is a strictly positive rational number. The identity (R1) and scalar extension give the stated witnesses over arbitrary characteristic-zero fields. For two negative levels use the larger dimension as \(n\). The level bound then applies unless the two dimensions are the same power of two. The square of the negative circle has Mathieu–Zhao trace by the Laurent theorem after algebraic extension, because each circle is a torus torsor.

For the anisotropy assertion use Hoffmann's separation theorem on \(\langle 1\rangle^{2^a}\) and \(\langle 1\rangle^{2^a+1}\). Over a formally real field both are anisotropic, their dimensions satisfy the separation inequality, and the function field of the latter projective quadric is the function field of the affine chart \(S_{2^a}^-\). In particular the relative frame cannot exist on the square of the norm-minus-one quaternion torsor over a real closed field. [Pfister's level theorem](https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/squares/2nidentities-and-the-stufe-of-fields-theorems-of-pfister-and-cassels/C9818E1699E911C0EC89B7EB7BE342E5) · [Hoffmann's separation theorem and its original reference](https://www.math.uni-bielefeld.de/LAG/man/129.pdf)

There is a low-degree pair for the first equal-dimensional case covered by the theorem. Take \(d=n=3\), or take \(d=3\) and any \(n\ge3\). In the first three coordinates take a fixed third unit vector \(e\) and set \(e_1=y\times e\), \(e_2=(y\times e)\times y\). These vectors are orthogonal and have opposite norms because \(q(y)=-1\). Now \(R=-1-y_3^2\). At \(y=i b\), the coordinate \(b_3\) is uniform on \([-1,1]\), giving
\[
 \Lambda_{S_3^-}(R^k)=(-1)^k\,\frac{k!}{(3/2)_k}.
\]
For \(S_3^\epsilon\times S_3^-\) itself \(F=P\), \(Q=U\), and the marked moment is \((-1)^m(m!/(3/2)_m)^2\). This includes the square of the negative adjoint sphere. Its simultaneous twist has compact commuting identity component. The construction uses variation of the split plane with the second variable. It does not decide the normalized trace on the negative adjoint sphere by itself.

### Toral cyclic twists

**Theorem (Toral cyclic twists as factors of regular witnesses).**

Let \(Y_j\) be the toral point-free form of \(\mathrm{SU}_2/\mu_j\) over a real closed field, with the nontrivial toral descent class. Its function field is a purely transcendental extension of degree two of \(K(S_2^-)\) if \(j\) is even. For \(j\) odd it is isomorphic to \(K(S_4^-)\). If \(j\) is even and \(n\ge3\), or \(j\) is odd and \(n\ge5\), \(S_n^\epsilon\times Y_j\) admits a regular counterexample with a fixed multiplier and a nonzero marked moment at every positive order, for either norm level \(\epsilon\).

For the function field use complexified first column coordinates \(u,v\) on the compact reference group, with \(u',v'\) their compact conjugates and \(uu'+vv'=1\). The two columns have opposite right torus weights. The twist conjugation on the degree-\(j\) holomorphic column monomials is minus compact conjugation, since the descent representative has \(j\)-th power \(-1\). On the pointed sphere \(C/D\), write \(A=uu'\). Its function field is a rational field \(F=K(s_1,s_2)\), with \(A=1/(1+s_1^2+s_2^2)\) in stereographic coordinates. The generic right torus quotient has split coordinate \(z=u^j\) over \(F(i)\). Twisted conjugation exchanges \(z\) with \(-A^j/z\). Hence its real function field is the field of the conic \(a^2+b^2=-A^j\) over \(F\). Dividing \(a,b\) by \(A^{\lfloor j/2\rfloor}\) gives the constant negative circle for even \(j\), and the same generic conic as \(j=1\) for odd \(j\). The latter reference twist is the negative quaternion norm quadric.

For the regular witness take the real-defined coordinates of each anti-conjugate pair \(g,g'\) to be \((g-g')/2,(g+g')/(2i)\). Their squared norm is \(-gg'\). If \(j\) is even, use \(g=u^j\) in this construction, embed the resulting two-vector as \(e_2\), and put \(e_1=A^{j/2}e\) for a fixed orthogonal unit vector \(e\). This gives \(R=A^j\). If \(j\) is odd, use the two monomials \(u^j,v u^{j-1}\). The resulting four-vector has norm \(-A^{j-1}\), so adjoining the perpendicular vector \(A^{(j-1)/2}e\) gives \(R=A^{j-1}\). These formulas are polynomial in quotient coordinates and use the actual twisted reality. In the compact reference \(A\) is uniform on \([0,1]\), since it is the sum of two squared coordinates on the unit sphere in four dimensions. The trace of \(R^k\) is \(1/(jk+1)\) in the even case and \(1/((j-1)k+1)\) in the odd case. The formula (R1) completes the regular calculation.

The birational comparisons here identify fields. The trace on the coordinate ring of an individual toral twist is still needed.

### A point criterion after taking a product with a sphere

**Theorem (Detecting point-free compact forms by sphere products).**

Let \(K\) be real closed, \(C\) a connected reductive group of compact type, and \(Y\) an affine geometrically homogeneous \(C\)-space of dimension \(d\). For every \(n\ge3\) with \(n>2^d\), the normalized trace on \(S_n^+\times Y\) is Mathieu–Zhao exactly when \(Y(K)\ne\varnothing\). If \(Y(K)=\varnothing\), the regular all-order pair already exists for every \(n\ge3\) exceeding the level of \(K(Y)\).

If \(Y\) has a point, pullback to the compact acting group proves strict square positivity, also on the product. If \(Y\) has no point, an ordering of \(K(Y)\) would give a point in a real closed extension of \(K\). The real closed field transfer principle applied to the affine equations would then give a point in \(Y(K)\). Thus \(K(Y)\) is not formally real. By Pfister's bound its level is at most \(2^d\), and for \(n\) exceeding this level the sum of \(n\) squares is isotropic over \(K(Y)\). [Pfister's level bound, as stated by Huisman](https://huisman.perso.math.cnrs.fr/rech/publications/lrac.pdf)

Choose the compact pointed reference \(Y_0\) and the commuting involution \(a\) from the compact-twist description. Write \(\sigma\) for reference conjugation, so that the point-free descent is \(a\sigma\). Choose a denominator \(L\in K[Y]\) for the rational hyperbolic pair, and replace it by \(M=L\,a^*L\). The automorphism \(a\) is defined on both forms and has order two. Thus \(M\) is invariant under \(a\) and real for both descents. It is a nonzero element of \(K[Y_0]\). The normalized traces on the two forms agree over \(K(i)\) by uniqueness on the common complexified homogeneous space. They have the same values on \(M^{2k}\), and these values are positive by strict square positivity on \(Y_0\). Taking \(R=M^2\) in (R1) proves the assertion without requiring a point on \(Y\).

For the negative ternary sphere this criterion gives the positive unit sphere in ambient dimension three as a detecting factor. For the norm-minus-one quaternion torsor it gives ambient dimension five. These dimensions are exact for the relative split-plane construction, by Hoffmann's separation theorem.

## Lifting regular counterexamples from a generic invariant fibre

**Theorem (A generic invariant fibre obstruction).**

Let \(K\) have characteristic zero, and let \(X\) be affine, geometrically integral, and geometrically homogeneous under a connected reductive group \(G\). Let \(S\subseteq G\) be a reductive subgroup, \(A=K[X]\), \(B=A^S\), and \(F=\operatorname{Frac} B\). The \(S\)-Reynolds projection \(R_S:A\to B\) extends to an \(F\)-linear functional \(R_F:F\otimes_B A\to F\). If \(\ker R_F\) fails to be Mathieu–Zhao in the generic fibre ring, then there are regular \(P_1,Q_1\in K[X]\) with zero pure moments at every positive order and nonzero marked moments at infinitely many orders for \(\Lambda_X\). The generic fibre and \(X\) may have no rational points.

Here is the denominator argument. Take a generic witness \(f,g\) and multiply each by a common nonzero element of \(B\) to obtain \(P,Q\in A\). By \(B\)-linearity and injectivity of \(B\to F\), \(R_S(P^m)=0\) for all \(m\ge1\), and \(c_m=R_S(QP^m)\in B\) is nonzero at infinitely many orders. Every \(h\in B\) preserves the pure identities on replacing the pair by \(hP,hQ\). The goal is to choose one \(h\) for which \(\Lambda_X(h^{m+1}c_m)\) survives infinitely often.

Descend the data, including \(P,Q\), to a finitely generated field \(E\subseteq K\). Invariant projection commutes with scalar extension, so both the vanishing and the nonvanishing orders of \(c_m\) hold in this model. Choose a finite-dimensional \(S\)-stable space of algebra generators of \(A_E\) and filter \(A_E\) by word degree in those generators. In each degree the filtration is \(S\)-stable, and the associated graded ring is finitely generated. Exactness of invariants gives
\[
                  \operatorname{gr}(A_E^S)=(\operatorname{gr} A_E)^S .
\]
The right hand side is finitely generated by reductive invariant theory. Lift homogeneous generators of positive degree to invariant elements \(b_1,\dots,b_v\in A_E^S\). By induction on degree, each invariant of filtration degree at most \(d\) is a polynomial in the \(b_i\) of weighted degree at most \(d\), with weights their filtration degrees. In particular it has ordinary degree at most \(d\) in them. Since \(R_S\) preserves the filtration, the ordinary degrees of the \(c_m\) in these generators are bounded by \(D_0(m+1)\) for a single \(D_0\).

Embed \(E\) in \(\mathbb C\). Choose the compact form of \(G_\mathbb C\) to contain a compact form of \(S_\mathbb C\), and choose a geometric base point whose stabilizer has a compatible compact form, as in the principal-orbit argument. The resulting compact conjugation on \(X_\mathbb C\) induces an antilinear involution \(*\) on the invariant ring. Its values on functions are ordinary complex conjugation along the compact orbit. Each \(b_i^*\) is a polynomial of bounded degree in the \(b_j\). Thus for one \(D\), all \(c_m^*\) have degree at most \(D(m+1)\). When \(c_m\ne0\), density of the compact orbit gives
\[
                           \Lambda_X(c_m c_m^*)>0
\]
on this model. Take a finite family \(h(\boldsymbol u)\) parametrizing the span of the monomials in the \(b_i\) of degree at most \(D\). The \((m+1)\)-st powers of this complex vector space span the polynomials of degree at most \(D(m+1)\) in the \(b_i\). To see this, partition a monomial into \(m+1\) factors of degree at most \(D\), then polarize the power. Therefore
\[
              a_m(\boldsymbol u)=\Lambda_X\bigl(h(\boldsymbol u)^{m+1} QP^m\bigr)
                               =\Lambda_X\bigl(h(\boldsymbol u)^{m+1}c_m\bigr)
\]
is a nonzero polynomial for each \(m\) with \(c_m\ne0\). Here the second equality uses \(\Lambda_X\circ R_S=\Lambda_X\).

Over \(E(\boldsymbol u)\) the sequence \(a_m\) is the mixed moment sequence of the regular pair \(hP,hQ\). After a finite extension of \(E\) providing a point of \(X\), the orbit map from \(G\) preserves these moments. Invariant volume and the scalar exponent-shift argument give a polynomial recurrence over the extension with a nonzero first coefficient. Expand its coefficients in a field basis and take a component that keeps a nonzero first coefficient to get a recurrence over \(E(\boldsymbol u)\). The arithmetic specialization lemma above then gives one \(\boldsymbol u\) over \(E\) with infinitely many nonzero marked terms. The identities \(R_S((hP)^m)=0\) hold at every specialization. This proves the assertion over \(E\) and over \(K\). [Loeser–Sabbah, algebraic Mellin transform](https://webusers.imj-prg.fr/~francois.loeser/LS_CMH.pdf)

**Theorem (A split rank-one obstruction over the invariant quotient field).**

If \(S\) above is connected and normal in \(G\), then the generic fibre is geometrically homogeneous under \(S_F\). In particular \(X\) has a regular counterexample if an \(F\)-split rank-one subgroup of \(S_F\) acts nontrivially on that fibre, even if \(S\) is anisotropic over \(K\).

Indeed, over a geometric field write \(X=G/H\). The invariant quotient by \(S\) is the homogeneous space \((G/S)/\operatorname{im} H\). To see the quotient ring, pull functions back to \(G\). They are left \(S\)-invariant and right \(H\)-invariant, hence descend to right \(\operatorname{im} H\)-invariants on \(G/S\). The projected subgroup has reductive identity component, so the homogeneous quotient is affine. Its fibres are \(S\)-orbits, as follows by lifting an element of \(\operatorname{im} H\) to \(H\). The description applies after algebraic closure of the generic field and the normalized \(S_F\)-trace is the localized Reynolds projection by uniqueness. The split rank-one theorem without rational points therefore gives a generic witness, and the preceding transfer globalizes it.

For a field-general example let \(D=(a,b)_K\) be a division quaternion algebra, \(E=K(\sqrt a)\), \(C=\mathrm{SL}_1(D)\), and \(Y=\{N_{E/K}(z)=b\}\). The latter is an affine torsor for the norm-one torus of \(E/K\). Over \(K(Y)\) its universal element \(z\) splits \(D\): represent \(E\) by multiplication on \(E\otimes_K K(Y)\) and the quaternion generator squaring to \(b\) by \(w\mapsto z\bar w\). These operators satisfy the defining relations and give an isomorphism with the matrix algebra. In particular for every affine geometrically homogeneous \(C\)-space \(Z\) with nontrivial action, the normalized trace on \(Z\times Y\) fails to be Mathieu–Zhao. The product has no \(K\)-point. When \(K\) is real closed, this includes both pointed exceptional quotients and every positive-dimensional compact rank-one twist as \(Z\), with the negative circle \(Y\). It also works when \(Y\) is any compact homogeneous form over a real closed field whose function field has level two, including the negative ternary sphere and the even toral cyclic twists. The same criterion applies to quotients with coupled stabilizers by taking the invariant quotient of the total space, without a product decomposition.

### An explicit binary-form pair over the norm torsor

**Theorem (Regular exceptional witnesses after a quaternion norm torsor).**

In the quaternion example let \(Z\) be affine and geometrically homogeneous under \(C=\mathrm{SL}_1(D)\), with geometric stabilizer of type \(2O\) or \(2I\). Then \(Z\times Y\) is isomorphic over \(Y\) to the product of \(Y\) with the corresponding split exceptional quotient, with actions identified by a splitting of \(D\) over \(K[Y]\). In particular the degree-eight or degree-twelve binary-form witness gives regular functions on \(Z\times Y\), with the original split marked moments. This applies also when \(Z(K)\) is empty.

Put \(B=K[Y]\). The operator \(w\mapsto z\bar w\) splits \(D\) over \(B\): it is defined on the free rank-two module \(E\otimes_K B\) and the algebra map to its endomorphisms is an isomorphism on every geometric fibre, hence over \(B\). The center of \(C\) acts trivially on \(Z\), because it belongs to each conjugate of the geometric stabilizer. Thus the acting adjoint group over \(B\) identifies with \(\mathrm{PGL}_{2,B}\).

Let \(M\) be the standard image of \(2O\) or \(2I\) in \(\mathrm{PGL}_2\), using the split binary-form stabilizer, and let \(Z_{\rm sp}=\mathrm{PGL}_2/M\). Over \(\overline K\) choose a fixed quaternion splitting \(\phi_0\) and a \(\phi_0\)-equivariant isomorphism \(Z_{\overline K}\simeq (Z_{\rm sp})_{\overline K}\). The splitting over \(B_{\overline K}\) differs from \(\phi_0\) by a \(B_{\overline K}\)-point \(A\) of \(\mathrm{PGL}_2\), using \(\operatorname{Aut}(M_2)=\mathrm{PGL}_2\). Composing the fixed isomorphism with left translation by \(A\) gives a regular equivariant isomorphism over \(Y_{\overline K}\) for the varying splitting. On each geometric fibre it is the unique equivariant isomorphism, because \(M\) is self-normalizing. All Galois conjugates of the isomorphism are therefore equal on every geometric point, so the isomorphism and its inverse descend to \(B\).

Pull back the fixed split pair along that isomorphism. On each fibre the conditional normalized trace agrees with the split trace by equivariance and uniqueness. Thus every pure and marked conditional moment is the corresponding scalar from the split quotient, and the remaining normalized trace on \(Y\) preserves it. In particular all the nonzero orders of the standard marked sequence survive. [Binary forms and the exceptional witnesses](https://hexagonmath.org/pdf/2611.00556v1)

## A prime-power test for mixed leading angular terms

**Theorem (A single-prime test for exceptional leading angular terms).**

Consider a regular Laurent phase \(P\) on either exceptional angular quotient, with coefficients in a number field. Take the weights \((2,3,9/2)\) on the octahedral \((p,q,J)\), and \((3,5,15/2)\) on the icosahedral \((p,r,J)\). Write the phase uniquely as an even part plus \(J\) times an even polynomial. Let \(d>0\) be the highest weight in that expression, and \(P_\infty(t,z)\) the Laurent polynomial obtained from its weight-\(d\) part by the substitutions below. Adjoin the substitution constants to the coefficient field if necessary. Choose a prime \(l\notin\{2,3,5\}\) with \(l\equiv1\bmod 4d\), and a place above \(l\) with residue field \(\mathbb F_l\) at which the coefficients and the constants of the substitutions are integral. Put \(m_1=(l-1)/(2d)\) and, in the residue field, set
\[
                  G(t)=\operatorname{CT}_z P_\infty(t,z)^{m_1}.
\]
If \(\operatorname{CT}_tG\ne0\) and the Laurent exponents of \(G\) are in \([-(l-1),l-1]\), then every pure moment of order
\[
                              m_j=\frac{l^j-1}{2d},\qquad j\ge1,
\]
is nonzero, with valuation \(-j\) when the valuation is normalized by \(v(l)=1\). The test allows mixed top angular coefficients and arbitrary lower angular terms. More generally, for any support of \(G\), a finite matrix over \(\mathbb F_l\) decides whether this valuation is attained infinitely often along the same orders.

In the octahedral case put
\[
 B=\frac{2+z+z^{-1}}4,\qquad U=\frac{z-z^{-1}}{4i},\qquad
 p_\infty=-\frac B3,\quad q_\infty=\frac{2B^2}{27},\quad
 J_\infty=\frac{2\sqrt2}{27}B^2 U .
\]
Here \(U^2=B(1-B)\). These are the leading terms of (H14) at \(S=\infty\), retaining the orientation sign. Indeed the discriminant expression gives
\[
 J^2=
 \frac{B(1-B)(S-2)^4(2S-1)^2(2BS-B+1)^2
                       (2BS^2-5BS+2B+S)^2}{5832 S^3}.
\]
The arcsine law with independent sign of \(J\) is the circle law used in the substitution. For even \(m\), the conditional trace is a Laurent polynomial in \(S\) with exponents between \(-dm/3\) and \(dm\). The bounds hold termwise: the lower exponents of \(p,q,J^2\) are at least \(0,-1,-3\), respectively, and odd powers of \(J\) disappear. Its coefficient at \(S^{dm}\) is \(\operatorname{CT}_z P_\infty^m\) before taking the remaining scalar constant term. The coefficients are integral away from \(2,3\), for integral input, since arcsine moments have only powers of two in their denominators. The final integration in uniform \(\sqrt S\) replaces \(S^k\) by
\[
                             \frac{2^{k+1}-2^{-k}}{2k+1}.
\]
At \(k=dm_j\) the denominator is \(l^j\), and the numerator is a unit since \(2^{l^j}-1\equiv1\bmod l\). All other denominators have strictly smaller valuation.

For the icosahedral case put \(u=(z+z^{-1})/2\) and \(v=(z-z^{-1})/(2i)\). The leading mirror law gives the substitutions
\[
 p_\infty=\frac{(1-u)(1+u)^2}{1080},\qquad
 r_\infty=-\frac{(1-u)^2(1+u)^3}{16200},\qquad
 J_\infty=4p_\infty^2 v .
\]
The independent orientation sign gives the same joint law as \((u,v)\) on the circle. For even \(m\) the conditional trace is polynomial in \(w\) of degree at most \(dm\) and coefficients integral away from \(2,3,5\). Its coefficient of \(w^{dm}\) is \(\operatorname{CT}_z P_\infty^m\). The factor from the final integration of \(w^k\) is \(1/(2k+1)\). Again at \(k=dm_j\) this has valuation \(-j\), uniquely among the terms. The congruence \(l\equiv1\bmod 4d\) makes all the chosen orders even, including when \(d\) is a half-integer.

In both cases \(P_\infty\) has angular Laurent exponents bounded by \(d\) in absolute value, as seen in the substitutions. The angular exponents in \(P_\infty^{m_1}\) consequently have absolute value at most \((l-1)/2\). In characteristic \(l\), Frobenius gives
\[
 P_\infty(t,z)^{m_j}
    =\prod_{a=0}^{j-1} P_\infty(t^{l^a},z^{l^a})^{m_1}.
\]
To reach degree zero in \(z\) the first angular exponent must be divisible by \(l\), hence zero. Repeating shows that every digit has angular degree zero. If \(|e|\le l-1\) also for every \(t\)-exponent in \(G\), the same argument for \(t\) shows
\[
                     \operatorname{CT}_{t,z}P_\infty^{m_j}
                                =(\operatorname{CT}_t G)^j
                     \quad\text{in }\mathbb F_l .
\]
For general \(G\) through degree bounds \(|e|\le H\), use the integer states \(a\) with \(|a|\le \lceil H/(l-1)\rceil\), and the matrix \(M_{a,b}=[t^{lb-a}]G(t)\). To have zero total exponent the successive digits satisfy \(a+e=lb\), with initial and final states zero. All states stay in the given bound. The constant term is \((M^j)_{0,0}\). The successive vectors \(M^j e_0\) over the finite field are eventually periodic, so the nonvanishing test is finite. Whenever it succeeds at an order, the highest angular term has valuation \(-j\) and cannot be canceled by a lower term.

For example the octahedral top part \(t^{-1}(1+t)^2(p^3+q^2)\) at \(l=73,m_1=6\) gives \(\operatorname{CT}_tG=35\bmod73\). The icosahedral top part \(t^{-1}(1+t)^2(p^5+r^3)\) at \(l=61,m_1=2\) gives \(\operatorname{CT}_tG=48\bmod61\). The values follow by expansion against the arcsine moments \(\mathbb E B^k=\binom{2k}{k}/4^k\) and \(\mathbb E u^{2k}=\binom{2k}{k}/4^k\), with the odd \(u\)-moments zero. They exclude these mixed leading terms and every perturbation of smaller angular weight at infinitely many orders, using a single prime for each example.

### A finite exclusion with an unrestricted quadratic product

**Theorem (Affine center and quadratic product exclusion).**

On either exceptional angular model, let (P=A+B/t+Ct), where the center (A) is affine in the two reflection invariants and the product (BC) is an arbitrary polynomial of degree at most two in them. If the first twelve positive moments vanish, then (A=0) and (BC=0). In particular the pole coefficient may be constant and the opposite coefficient genuinely quadratic. The conclusion also holds on replacing (t) by (t^e) for any positive integer (e).

The first moment centers (A). Use the full eight-variable system (H7) through order twelve, omitting the determinant equation. Formula (H3) or (H6) computes its coefficients, and the odd factors in the final height moment are bounded by (73) or (121), respectively. The coefficients are therefore integral at (131). In the same variable order as before, the leading ideal over (mathbb F_{131}) contains pure powers of exponents
[
                             (2,4,6,6,6,7,8,16)
]
on both models. The degree-reverse-lexicographic basis computation is included in the [replay checker](verify_exceptional_affine_laurent_obstruction.py). Weighted homogeneity gives only the zero geometric solution, and the weighted valuation argument transfers that conclusion to every characteristic-zero field. The domain property then leaves just one strict Laurent sign whenever the phase is nonzero.

## Reflection descent from the quaternion norm torsor

The split product admits a trace-preserving copy of the reflection quotient of the negative circle. Write \(Y^-=\{u^2+v^2=-1\}\) and \(\iota(u,v)=(u,-v)\). Its invariant ring is \(K[u]\). Sending \(u\) to \((t-t^{-1})/2\) in the split Laurent ring preserves the trace, since in both models \(u\) has zero odd moments and even moments \((-1)^m\binom{2m}{m}/4^m\). In particular, a regular counterexample on \(X_\Gamma\times Y^-\) whose phase is \(\iota\)-invariant gives one on \(X_\Gamma\times\mathbb G_m\). The multiplier can be made invariant by its two-term average, which preserves every marked moment when the phase is invariant.

**Theorem (Reflection moments of the transported exceptional pair).**

Let \(X_\Gamma\) be a pointed compact binary octahedral or icosahedral quotient over \(\mathbb R\), and use the quaternion norm-torsor isomorphism with \(Y^-\). Let \(f\) be a nonzero real regular pure phase on the split exceptional quotient, \(F\) its transport to \(X_\Gamma\times Y^-\), and \(F^\iota\) its reflected transport. Then \(F F^\iota\) and every linear combination \(aF+bF^\iota\) with \(ab\ne0\) have a nonzero pure moment. In particular the norm and the reflection average of the transported phase cannot supply the reflection-invariant witness.

To compute the reflection action, split \((-1,-1)\) over \(Y^-\) by sending its generators to
\[
   J_0=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
   L=\begin{pmatrix}u&v\\v&-u\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Reflection changes the splitting by conjugation by \(A=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)L\), since \(A\) commutes with \(J_0\) and \(ALA^{-1}\) replaces \(v\) by \(-v\). Uniqueness of the equivariant quotient isomorphism then identifies reflection with left multiplication by \(A\) over the reflected point of the base. On the compact averaging cycle \(u+iv=e^{i\theta}\), \(u-iv=-e^{-i\theta}\), the class of \(A\) is the class of
\[
                 A/i=\begin{pmatrix}\sin\theta&-\cos\theta\\
                                      \cos\theta&\sin\theta\end{pmatrix}.
\]
It is uniform on the rotation torus in the split adjoint group. Let \(R_0\) denote its normalized averaging operator on the split quotient, and \(g_n=R_0(f^n)\). Product trace and invariance therefore give the exact identity
\[
                     \Lambda(F^a(F^\iota)^b)=\lambda(g_a g_b)
                     \qquad(a,b\ge0),                         \tag{R3}
\]
where \(\lambda\) is the normalized trace on the split exceptional quotient.

The standard binary-form stabilizers both contain \(J_0\), as seen by substituting \((Y,-X)\) in the octic and dodecic. Compact conjugation of the split quotient is split conjugation followed by left translation by \(J_0\). Thus \(g_n\) is real on the compact reference orbit. Some \(g_r\), \(r\ge1\), is nonzero. Otherwise restrict \(f\) to a rotation-torus orbit of a compact reference point where \(f\ne0\). Its Laurent exponents occur in opposite pairs because complex conjugation of the values along the circle is translation by \(J_0^{-1}\), which preserves the exponents. The one-variable Laurent theorem prevents all the constant moments from vanishing. For the least such \(r\), compact square positivity gives \(\lambda(g_r^2)>0\). By (R3) the \(r\)-th norm moment is \(\lambda(g_r^2)\). The \(2r\)-th moment of \(aF+bF^\iota\) is \(\binom{2r}{r}(ab)^r\lambda(g_r^2)\): terms with both exponents positive and one below \(r\) are zero by minimality, and the two pure end terms vanish by purity of \(f\). This proves both obstructions.

## Legendre moments on the negative ternary sphere

**Theorem (A bounded rational Legendre witness on the negative sphere).**

On \(X=\{x_1^2+x_2^2+h^2=-1\}\), put \(z=x_1+i x_2\) and \(z'=x_1-i x_2\), and take the real rational functions
\[
 P=h+\frac12\left(\frac{z^2}{1+i h}+\frac{z'^2}{1-i h}\right),\qquad
 Q_1=\frac{z^2+z'^2}{1+h^2},\qquad
 Q_2=\frac{i(z^2-z'^2)}{1+h^2}.
\]
They are bounded almost everywhere on the compact reference cycle \(X(\mathbb C)\supset i S^2\). The normalized compact trace of \(P^m\) vanishes for every \(m\ge1\), and \(Q_1+Q_2\) detects every positive order. For every polynomial marker \(U\) on the affine quadric, the marked moment of \(P^m\) vanishes when \(m>\deg U\), using any polynomial representative of \(U\). The bound is sharp at each order.

On \(iS^2\) write \(h=i x\), with \(x\) uniform on \([-1,1]\), and \((x_1,x_2)=i\sqrt{1-x^2}(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\), with independent uniform \(\theta\). At \(s=e^{2i\theta}\),
\[
 P=i x-\frac{1+x}{2}s-\frac{1-x}{2}s^{-1},\qquad
 Q_1=-s-s^{-1},\qquad Q_2=-i(s-s^{-1}).
\]
In particular only the two polar points of the compact cycle are excluded from the coordinate calculation, and all these functions are bounded on the rest. Write \(\mathcal L_m\) for the Legendre polynomial with \(\mathcal L_m(1)=1\). The circle constant term of \(P^m\) is \(i^m\mathcal L_m(x)\). For \(k\ge0\) the angular coefficients are
\[
 \operatorname{CT}_s(s^{\pm k}P^m)
   =i^{m+k}\frac{m!}{(m+k)!}(1\mp x)^k\mathcal L_m^{(k)}(x).
                                                               \tag{R4}
\]
Indeed, first balance the two angular coefficients by a scalar change of \(s\). The balanced power is a constant multiple of \((x+i\sqrt{1-x^2}\cos(2\theta))^m\), a spherical harmonic of degree \(m\) since the defining linear form is isotropic. Its Fourier coefficient of weight \(k\) is proportional to \((1-x^2)^{k/2}\mathcal L_m^{(k)}(x)\). The limit at \(x=1\), using \(\mathcal L_m^{(k)}(1)=(m+k)!/[2^k k!(m-k)!]\) for \(k\le m\), gives the prefactor. Undoing the balancing gives (R4). [Spherical harmonics and Legendre polynomials](https://dlmf.nist.gov/14.30)

Orthogonality against constants gives the pure moments. For the two markers, (R4) and the endpoints \(\mathcal L_m(\pm1)=(\pm1)^m\) give
\[
 \Lambda(Q_1P^m)=-\frac{i^{m+1}}{m+1}\bigl(1-(-1)^m\bigr),\qquad
 \Lambda(Q_2P^m)= \frac{i^{m+2}}{m+1}\bigl(1+(-1)^m\bigr).
\]
For a polynomial marker it is enough to take \(h^b z^\alpha z'^\beta\). An odd difference \(\alpha-\beta\) cannot meet the phase's even azimuthal support. Otherwise set \(k=|\alpha-\beta|/2\) and \(j=\min(\alpha,\beta)\). Formula (R4) gives the integral of \(\mathcal L_m^{(k)}(x)\) against a scalar times \(x^b (1-x^2)^{j+k}(1\mp x)^k\). The weight vanishes to order at least \(k\) at both endpoints, so \(k\) integrations by parts give a polynomial weight of degree at most \(b+2j+2k=b+\alpha+\beta\). Orthogonality proves eventual vanishing with the stated bound. The regular marker \(h^m\) has moment \((-1)^m 2^m(m!)^2/(2m+1)!\), by Rodrigues integration by parts, so the least possible regular marker degree at order \(m\) is \(m\).

**Theorem (Clearing the Legendre phase by a height polynomial destroys purity).**

In the function field of the negative ternary sphere the polynomial ring in \(h,P\) meets the regular ring exactly in \(K[h,(1+h^2)P]\), over any characteristic-zero field \(K\). Put \(S=(1+h^2)P\). Every nonconstant phase in \(K[h,S]\) with a unique highest monomial in weights \(1,3\) has infinitely many nonzero positive moments. This includes every nonzero height-polynomial clearing \(f(h)P\).
