Use the isomorphism torsor \(X=\operatorname{Isom}_{K\text{-alg}}(A,B)\) and centralizer quotient of the preceding construction, with \(A,B\) of degree \(n\) and with \(E/K\) Galois inside \(A\). Put \(r=n/[E:K]\ge2\). The derived subgroup of the precomposition centralizer has positive split rank over the generic invariant quotient field precisely when
\[
             \gcd_{1\le i\le n}
                \left\{\frac{r}{\gcd(i,r)}
                          \operatorname{ind}(A_E\otimes B_E^{\otimes i})\right\}
                            < r .
\]
In that case \(X\) admits a regular pure phase with a fixed regular multiplier whose marked moments are nonzero infinitely often. In particular this applies to a new family where the centralizer algebra becomes a matrix algebra over a division algebra of smaller index that still exceeds one.

Indeed the generic quotient field after extending constants to \(E\) is purely transcendental over \(E(\mathrm{SB}_r(B_E))\), by the ordered decomposition of right ideals used above. The index on that function field is the displayed gcd, by the generalized Severi–Brauer index formula (with \(r\mid n\)). A purely transcendental extension preserves index, by specialization or by embedding into a Laurent series field. The centralizer algebra \(C_{F_E}\) therefore has the displayed index \(d\) and degree \(r\). If \(d<r\), then \(C_{F_E}\simeq M_{r/d}(D')\) over a division algebra \(D'\), and the scalar operations on two summands give an \(\mathrm{SL}_{2,F_E}\) in its norm-one group. The scalar copy of \(\mathrm{SL}_{2,F}\) in the restriction of scalars acts nontrivially on the generic fibre by precomposition, hence lifts to a regular counterexample by the generic-orbit theorem. If \(d=r\), the norm-one group over \(F_E\) is anisotropic. Its restriction of scalars and its central image remain anisotropic, as in the split criterion above. [Karpenko, *Three theorems on common splitting fields of central simple algebras*, formula in Lemma III.2](https://sites.ualberta.ca/~karpenko/publ/lnk.pdf)

For example let \(n=2^h\) with \(h\ge3\), \(r=n/2\), and take \(K=\mathbb Q(\zeta_n)(u,v,s,t)\),
\[
                    B=(u,v)_n,\qquad A=(u,v)_r\otimes_K(s,t)_2 ,
                   \qquad E=K(\sqrt{s}) .
\]
Both \(A,B\) are division over \(K\) by the disjoint coordinate valuations, with periods \(r,n\) respectively. Over \(E\) the first Brauer class is \(2[B_E]\), and \(B_E\) still has period \(n\), by the same valuation of monomials with denominators in the \(u,v\) directions. The term \(i=n-2\) bounds the displayed index by \(r/2\), since \(2[B_E]+(n-2)[B_E]=0\). Conversely, the Brauer kernel of the quotient extension over \(E\) is \(\langle r[B_E]\rangle\), so \(2[B_E]\) has period \(r/2\) there, giving equality of the index with \(r/2\). In particular the centralizer does not split over this quotient field, and the partial index drop supplies the split rank-one action. The original torsor is point-free and both original acting forms are anisotropic.


## A flat quaternion height and its conditional Poisson law

**Theorem (A regular squared-uniform height on the negative quaternion torsor).**

Let \(X_-=\{x:N(x)=-1\}\) for the Hamilton quaternion algebra over a real closed field. Write \(x=a+x_1i+x_2j+x_3k\), with \(i,j,k\) the quaternion generators. Then
\[
                     D=-\bigl((x^3)_2^2+(x^3)_3^2\bigr)
                       =-(4a^2+1)^2(x_2^2+x_3^2)
\]
has the squared-uniform trace law. In particular on \(X_-\times\mathbb G_m\), with the split scalar coordinate \(t\), there is a regular all-order bad pair
\[
       P=t^{-1}(1+t)\bigl(1-(1+t)^2D\bigr)^2,\qquad Q=t .
\]

In the flat quaternion formula for \(x^3\), write the compact-reference coordinates as \(i_{\mathbb C}(\cos\theta,\sin\theta\,n_1,\sin\theta\,n_2,\sin\theta\,n_3)\). Here \(i_{\mathbb C}^2=-1\) is scalar, \(\theta\) is uniform on the circle, and \(n\) is uniform on the two-sphere independently of \(\theta\). Thus \(D=\sin^2\theta(1-n_1^2)\). Its moments are
\[
                     \frac{(1/2)_m}{m!}\frac{m!}{(3/2)_m}
                                    =\frac1{2m+1}.
\]
On the torsor \(x^2-2ax-1=0\), so the asserted degree-six regular expression for \(D\) follows from \(x^3=(4a^2+1)x+2a\). Put \(T=1+t\) and \(I_m(T)=\int_0^T(1-z^2)^{2m}\,dz\). Averaging the \(m\)-th power on \(X_-\) gives \(t^{-m}T^{m-1}I_m(T)\). The endpoint difference \(I_m(1+t)-I_m(1)\) is divisible by \(t^{2m+1}\). The polynomial \(T^{m-1}\) then gives zero pure moment and \(Q\)-marked moment
\[
                     I_m(1)=\frac{4^{2m}((2m)!)^2}{(4m+1)!}.
\]
All moment identities have rational coefficients and transfer to real closed fields by base change.

**Theorem (Poisson fibres of the flat quaternion trace).**

Use \(a,x_1,x_2,x_3\) for the coordinates under the flat trace before the power pullback. Write \(r=\sqrt{-(x_2^2+x_3^2)}\) on its compact reference cycle. The variable \(r\) is uniform on \([0,1]\). The angle in the \((x_2,x_3)\)-plane is uniform and independent of the other coordinates. Conditional on \(r\in(0,1)\), put \(\rho=\sqrt{1-r^2}\) and \((a,x_1)=i_{\mathbb C}\rho(\cos\gamma,\sin\gamma)\). The law of \(\gamma\) has density
\[
                     \frac{1-q^2}{1-2q\cos(2\gamma)+q^2}
                                      \frac{d\gamma}{2\pi},
                     \qquad q=\frac{1-r}{1+r}.
\]
For this fixed-radius law no nonzero real Laurent phase in the complementary angle can have all pure moments zero. More generally the complex Laurent phases pure for this law are exactly the polynomials in the circle variable or its inverse that vanish at both Poisson centres for that variable, including zero.

To compute the density, start with \(u=\cos\theta\), \(v=\sin\theta\,n_1\), on the real reference sphere. The first coordinate has arcsine density, and \(v\) is uniform on \([-\sqrt{1-u^2},\sqrt{1-u^2}]\) conditional on \(u\). Thus their joint density is \(du\,dv/(2\pi(1-u^2))\) on the disk. The angle of \((n_2,n_3)\) is independent and uniform. Passing to polar coordinates \(u+iv=\rho e^{i\gamma}\) gives \(r\,dr\,d\gamma/(2\pi(1-(1-r^2)\cos^2\gamma))\), which is the stated Poisson density.

For the purity assertion set \(z=e^{i\gamma}\) and
\[
                     y=\frac{z^2-q}{1-qz^2},\qquad
                     \eta=\frac12\,\frac{dy}{y}.
\]
The weighted circle integral is \((2\pi i_{\mathbb C})^{-1}\int_{|z|=1}P(z)^m\eta\). If a Laurent phase \(P\) has poles at both \(0,\infty\), let \(\mathcal J\) be the inverse branches of \(P(z)=u\) tending to \(z=0\) as \(u\to\infty\). With \(d=|\mathcal J|\), write \(y(z)=c\exp g(u^{-1/d})\) at \(z=0\). The product of the \(y\)-values on \(\mathcal J\) has expansion
\[
              H(u)=c^d\exp\!\left(\sum_{m\ge1}
                      \frac{\operatorname{Res}_{z=0}P(z)^m\,dy/y}{m u^m}\right).
\]
If the moments vanish, the residues at the two simple zeros \(z=\pm\sqrt q\) of \(y\) give
\[
                      H(u)=c^d\bigl(1-P(\sqrt q)/u\bigr)
                                   \bigl(1-P(-\sqrt q)/u\bigr).
\]
Thus \(H\) is rational. On the branches near \(0\) the limiting \(y\)-value is \(-q\), while on those near \(\infty\) it is \(-q^{-1}\). The product on \(\mathcal J\) is distinguished by its limiting value from the products on all other subsets of the same size: replacing any selected branch changes that value by a nontrivial power of \(q^{-2}\). Rationality of \(H\) therefore forces \(\mathcal J\) to be invariant under the transitive inverse-branch group, contradicting the presence of poles at both ends.

If \(P\) is polynomial in \(z\), the moment is just the mean of \(P(\sqrt q)^m,P(-\sqrt q)^m\). Their simultaneous vanishing for all powers forces both values to be zero. The case of a polynomial in \(z^{-1}\) follows by inversion, using the two centres for the inverted coordinate. On the negative reference fibre the real involution sends \(z\) to \(-z^{-1}\) and conjugates coefficients. Hence the support of a real Laurent phase is symmetric. The one-sided alternatives then give only the zero phase.

This Poisson law gives an explicit complex all-order witness on the negative quaternion torsor with a real multiplier. In flat coordinates put
\[
           F=(a+i_{\mathbb C}x_1)^2(1+a^2),\qquad
           G=(a-i_{\mathbb C}x_1)^2+(a+i_{\mathbb C}x_1)^2 .
\]
The phase \(F\) is polynomial in \(z^2\) on each Poisson fibre and vanishes at \(z=\pm\sqrt q\). In fact
\[
       F=-\rho^2 z^2+\frac{\rho^4}{4}(z^4+2z^2+1),
       \qquad
       F(0)=\rho^4/4.
\]
For the marked trace the positive \(z^2\) part of \(G\) gives zero. Its other part is \(-\rho^2 z^{-2}\), producing just the residue at zero, of value \(4r(\rho^4/4)^m\). Integrating the uniform \(r\) gives
\[
                  \Lambda_{\rm flat}(F^m)=0,\qquad
                  \Lambda_{\rm flat}(GF^m)=\frac{2}{4^m(2m+1)} .
\]
Composition with \(x\mapsto x^3\) gives these identities on the original norm-minus-one torsor over \(K(i_{\mathbb C})\). The phase has complex coefficients. The real ground-field problem on \(X_-\) alone allows cancellations between different radii and is outside the conditional classification.

## A restriction on full-quotient phases with two left weights

**Theorem (An opposite left-weight pair reduces to an angular product).**

Let \(X=\mathrm{SO}(3)/\Gamma\) be either full exceptional compact quotient and use one left maximal circle. Work on \(X\times\mathbb G_m\), where the split scalar is denoted \(t\). Suppose that a regular phase \(P\) has just the two opposite nonzero weights \(h,-h\) for the effective left circle. Write \(P=U+V\) with \(U,V\) in those weight spaces and put \(R=UV\), a regular function on the angular quotient times \(\mathbb G_m\). Then \(P\) is pure exactly when \(R\) is pure. For a pure phase \(P\), it is safe against every fixed regular multiplier exactly when \(R\) is safe on the angular quotient times \(\mathbb G_m\). Here safety means eventual vanishing of the marked moments.

In particular, if \(P\) is real in either exceptional model, the family of its left-circle and angular conditional even moments cannot have a separated radial law at generic real \(t\). On the icosahedral mirror chart, the normalized conditional moment field of \(P\), before extracting the scalar constant term, determines the original height algebraically. The same assertion holds on the octahedral joint chart whenever \(R\) has a nonzero orientation-odd part. These exclude transport of the entire indicated law from \(T^2w\) or \(T^2S\) alone for such full-quotient phases, where \(T=1+t\).

Indeed left-circle averaging gives \(R_L(P^{2m})=\binom{2m}{m}R^m\) and \(R_L(P^{2m+1})=0\). This proves the purity equivalence. For a fixed multiplier \(Q\), split it into finitely many circle weights over the complexification. If \(Q_j\) has weight \(j\), only the binomial term whose \(U\)-count minus \(V\)-count is \(d=-j/h\) contributes. If \(d\) is integral and the parity fits, this is a scalar binomial coefficient times
\[
                   R^{(m-|d|)/2}\,Q_j U^{\max(d,0)} V^{\max(-d,0)}
\]
at power \(m\), with the factor after \(R^{(m-|d|)/2}\) a fixed angular multiplier. Thus safety of \(R\) implies safety of \(P\), also over the real field by linearity. Conversely take an angular multiplier for \(R\) and use it unchanged on the even powers of \(P\).

For real \(P\), the two coefficients \(U,V\) are compact conjugates. The angular \(R\) cannot be scalar Laurent in \(t\): after passing to the generic scalar field, this would make \(U\) a unit regular on the compact homogeneous quotient, and its pullback to the semisimple group has only constant units. This contradicts the nonzero weight. If \(C_{2m}^P\) denotes the doubly conditional even moment of \(P\) and \(C_m^R\) the angular conditional moment of \(R\), their relation is \(C_{2m}^P=\binom{2m}{m}C_m^R\). Hence the normalized field of the \(C_{2m}^P\) contains all \(C_{2m}^R/(C_2^R)^m\), by taking the appropriate ratios of \(C_{4m}^P\) and \(C_4^P\). A separated radial law for \(P\) would give one for \(R\), which has been excluded. The conditional height results for \(R\) give the two height assertions. Purity and safety here concern the final integrated traces; the separation and transport obstructions concern only the conditional moments.

## Pfister neighbours in oriented isometry torsors

**Theorem (A Pfister witness on a point-free isometry torsor).**

Let \(q_1,q_2\) be nondegenerate \(n\)-dimensional quadratic forms over a field \(K\) of characteristic zero, with \(n\ge4\) and determinant ratio a square. Fix a determinant component \(X=\operatorname{Isom}^{\rm or}(q_1,q_2)\). Suppose that \(q_1=\langle c\rangle\perp q_1'\), that \(q_2\perp\langle-c\rangle\) is a subform of a Pfister form \(\pi\), and that \(q_1'\) contains a subform similar to a subform of \(\pi\) of dimension strictly greater than \(\frac12\dim\pi\). Then \(X\) has a regular pure phase with a fixed regular multiplier whose marked moments are nonzero infinitely often. This can occur with both orthogonal groups anisotropic over \(K\) and \(X(K)=\varnothing\).

The subgroup \(S=\mathrm{SO}(q_1')\) acts by precomposition. The map taking an isometry to the image of the chosen vector of norm \(c\) identifies the invariant quotient with \(\{v:q_2(v)=c\}\). Over an algebraic closure, an isometry of that line extends to the fixed determinant component, and each fibre is an \(S\)-torsor. This gives the affine invariant quotient over \(K\) by base change. Over its function field \(F\), \(q_2\perp\langle-c\rangle\) is isotropic. The Pfister form \(\pi_F\) is therefore hyperbolic. Every subform of a scalar multiple of \(\pi_F\) of dimension greater than half meets a maximal totally isotropic subspace. In particular \(q_1'\) is isotropic over \(F\). Its special orthogonal group has a split rank-one subgroup, acting nontrivially on the generic \(S\)-torsor. The generic-orbit theorem supplies the regular witness over \(K\). [Lam, *Introduction to Quadratic Forms over Fields*, Theorem X.1.7](https://bookstore.ams.org/gsm-67)

For a point-free example of dimension \(11\), take \(K=\mathbb Q(u_1,u_2,u_3,u_4)\) and \(\pi=\bigotimes_{i=1}^4\langle1,u_i\rangle\). Index its diagonal monomials \(u^v\) by \(v\in(\mathbb F_2)^4\) with coordinate vectors \(e_i\). Let \(q_2\) use all indices except \(0,e_1,e_2,e_1+e_3,e_2+e_3\). Let \(Q_0\) use the nine indices
\[
         \bigl(\langle e_1,e_2,e_3\rangle\setminus\{0,e_1\}\bigr)
                 \cup\{e_4,e_4+e_2,e_4+e_3\},
\]
and set \(\sigma=e_1+e_2+e_3+e_4\) and \(q_1=\langle-1\rangle\perp Q_0\perp\langle-u^\sigma\rangle\). The sum of the omitted indices for \(q_2\) is zero. The sum of the nine indices in \(Q_0\) is \(\sigma\), which is not among them. Both determinants are squares. In an iterated Laurent series extension in the \(u_i\), the diagonal coefficient valuations of each form are distinct modulo \(2\mathbb Z^4\), so any nonzero vector gives a unique lowest-valuation term of its norm. Thus both forms are anisotropic already over \(K\). In an ordering with all \(u_i>0\), \(q_2\) cannot represent \(-1\), although \(q_1\) does. The isometry torsor is therefore point-free. With \(c=-1\), \(q_2\perp\langle1\rangle\) is a subform of \(\pi\) and \(Q_0\) is a nine-dimensional subform of the sixteen-dimensional Pfister form. The theorem applies.


## A zero-weight term in the full exceptional products

**Lemma (Normalized even moments detect a scalar centre).**

Let \(u,v\) be two independent real parameters in an open region. Suppose a family of compactly supported real laws is symmetric about a rational centre \(a(u,v)\), and its moments \(C_j(u,v)\) are rational functions. Assume \(C_2>0\) in the region. Then
\[
                     a^2/C_2
          \quad\text{is algebraic over}\quad
                     M=\mathbb C(C_{2m}/C_2^m:m\ge1).
\]
For a single real parameter, a constant normalized even law similarly forces \(a^2/C_2\) to be constant on each connected interval.

Indeed the normalized cosine transform of each law is an entire function \(\Phi_{u,v}(\zeta)\), with Taylor coefficients determined by the normalized even moments. Symmetry about the centre gives
\[
                \Phi_{u,v}(\zeta)=
                  \cos\!\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{C_2}}\zeta\right)
                            \Phi_{u,v}^{\,0}(\zeta),
\]
where \(\Phi_{u,v}^{\,0}\) is the cosine transform after centring and scaling. If the normalized even moments are fixed along an arc, the first factor cannot have a varying nonzero \(a^2/C_2\) there: its varying real zeros would have an accumulation point among the zeros of a fixed entire function with value one at zero.

For the field assertion, all relevant rational functions have real coefficients. If \(M\) has transcendence degree zero, use this arc argument directly. If it has transcendence degree two, the claim is automatic. Otherwise choose a nonconstant real rational \(b\in M\). If \(a^2/C_2\) were transcendental over \(M\), the real map \((b,a^2/C_2)\) would have nonzero Jacobian on an open subset. Use these as local real coordinates there, and take a connected arc with \(b\) fixed at a regular real value. Every normalized even moment is algebraic over \(\mathbb C(b)\). Choosing the value of \(b\) outside the countable exceptional set of zeros of the leading coefficients of these algebraic relations makes each moment take finitely many possible values on that arc. They are continuous and consequently constant there. The varying second coordinate then contradicts the cosine-transform argument. The one-parameter assertion follows from the same argument with a fixed transform.

**Theorem (A scalar centre preserves the full-quotient height obstruction).**

Let \(P=a(t)+U+V\) be real and regular on either full exceptional compact quotient times the split scalar \(t\), with \(U,V\) a nonzero pair of opposite left-circle weights and with \(a(t)\) scalar Laurent. Put \(R=UV\), regular on the angular quotient times the split scalar. Form the field \(M\) from the normalized conditional even moments of \(P\) after averaging the left circle and the conditional angular law, before extracting the constant term in \(t\).

On the icosahedral mirror chart \(w\) is algebraic over \(M\). On the octahedral joint chart \(S\) is algebraic over \(M\) if \(R\) has a nonzero orientation-odd component. In either chart, at generic fixed real \(t\), the normalized conditional even moments cannot be independent of the height. Thus adding a scalar centre to the opposite left pair does not restore the separated law, and in the stated height-field cases it does not allow transport of the normalized even law from the dilated height alone.

For real \(t\) and physical height, the conditional law of \(P\) is symmetric about \(a(t)\): a half-period of the active left weight changes \(U+V\) to its negative. Put \(Z=a^2/C_2^P\). The preceding lemma makes \(Z\) algebraic over \(M\). If \(C_j^R\) is the angular conditional moment of \(R\), the binomial formula gives
\[
       \frac{C_{2m}^P}{(C_2^P)^m}
          =\sum_{j=0}^m \binom{2m}{2j}\binom{2j}{j} Z^{m-j}
                                   \frac{C_j^R}{(C_2^P)^j}.
\]
The nonzero diagonal coefficients recover all normalized even moments of \(R\) in \(M(Z)\), by triangular inversion and division by the indicated second moment of \(R\). The angular \(R\) has genuine angular dependence, by the constant-unit argument for opposite weight pairs. The previously proved height-field tests for \(R\) give the first two conclusions. For the fixed-\(t\) assertion apply the one-parameter lemma and the same triangular inversion, followed by the exceptional angular nonseparation theorem. All these arguments concern conditional moments; the remaining final height integral and Laurent extraction are part of the original purity problem.

## Real odd harmonics in the flat quaternion model

**Theorem (A one-dimensional arcsine mixture with imaginary amplitude is not pure).**

Let \(a,b\) be real analytic functions on a neighbourhood of a compact interval with positive continuous probability density. With \(\theta\) independently uniform on a circle, the phase
\[
                              a(r)+i b(r)\cos\theta
\]
has a nonzero positive moment unless both functions are identically zero. The same proof allows a signed amplitude \(b\) that is analytic locally up to sign at its zeros.

The cases \(b\equiv0\) or \(a\equiv0\) follow from the second moment. Otherwise suppose purity. For large positive \(z\), expansion of the resolvent and circle integration give
\[
                         I(z)=\int
                   \frac{d\mu(r)}{\sqrt{(z-a(r))^2+b(r)^2}}=\frac1z ,
\]
with the positive square root for real \(z\). If \(b\) has no zeros, the positive integral extends real analytically through the whole real axis and contradicts the pole of \(1/z\). Otherwise let \(c\) be the largest value of \(a\) at a zero of \(b\). The positive integral is real analytic for \(z>c\). Thus \(c<0\) is again excluded by its regularity at zero. If \(c>0\), at a point \(r_0\) with \(a(r_0)=c,\ b(r_0)=0\) the square root is bounded above by a constant times \((z-c)+|r-r_0|\). With positive density, this makes \(I(z)\) diverge as \(z\downarrow c\), contradicting \(1/z\).

Finally let \(c=0\). We show \(zI(z)\to0\) as \(z\downarrow0\). Away from the finitely many common zeros this is immediate. At a common zero put \(v=r-r_0\), and let \(k,\ell\ge1\) be the orders of \(a,b\). If \(\ell\le k\), the square root bounds \(z+|v|^\ell\) from below up to a positive factor: it bounds \(|b|\), and \(z\le|z-a|+|a|\) with \(|a|\lesssim |b|\). Dominated convergence applies. If \(\ell>k\), outside the range \(z/2\le a(r)\le2z\) one has \(|z-a|\gtrsim z+|a|\), giving the same conclusion. Inside that range, \(|v|\asymp z^{1/k}\), \(|b|\gtrsim z^{\ell/k}\), and \(|a'|\gtrsim z^{(k-1)/k}\). On the at most two local monotone branches, substitute \(u=a(r)\). Their contributions to \(zI(z)\) are bounded by a constant times
\[
                        z^{1/k}\int_{z/2}^{2z}
                        \frac{du}{\sqrt{(z-u)^2+c_0 z^{2\ell/k}}}
                          = O\bigl(z^{1/k}(1+|\log z|)\bigr)
\]
with \(c_0>0\). This also works with a one-sided interval at an endpoint. Thus \(zI(z)\to0\), contrary to purity.

For the flat quaternion law put \(D=-(x_2^2+x_3^2)\). A real regular phase of the form
\[
           A(D)+B(D)\operatorname{Re}_{K}\bigl((x_2+i_{\mathbb C}x_3)^h\bigr)
               +C(D)\operatorname{Im}_{K}\bigl((x_2+i_{\mathbb C}x_3)^h\bigr)
\]
with \(A,B,C\) polynomials and an integer \(h\ge1\) has a nonzero pure moment unless it is zero. Here the real and imaginary parts mean coefficient conjugation in the ground-field polynomial algebra. If \(h\) is even, the phase takes real values on the compact reference cycle and square positivity applies. If \(h\) is odd, the circle at fixed \(r=\sqrt D\) gives an imaginary arcsine term with amplitude \(r^h\sqrt{B(r^2)^2+C(r^2)^2}\); this is locally analytic up to sign at its zeros. The flat radius is uniform, so the theorem applies. The moments are polynomial in the coefficients, so their simultaneous vanishing in any fixed degree gives a finitely defined system and the exclusion transfers to real closed fields. It also holds for their odd-power pullbacks to the negative quaternion torsor.

**Lemma (A fixed-divisor norm obstruction for a descended Legendre phase).**

In the flat quaternion function field adjoin \(b\) with \(b^2=x_2^2+x_3^2\), and write
\[
       c=x_2/b,\quad d=x_3/b,\quad r=-i_{\mathbb C}b,\quad
       z^2=-\frac{(a+i_{\mathbb C}x_1)^2}{1+b^2},\quad
       w=\frac{z^2-(1-r)/(1+r)}{1-z^2(1-r)/(1+r)} .
\]
Here \(c^2+d^2=1\); the Poisson uniformizer \(w\) is defined over the real field of the double cover because conjugation inverts \(z^2\) and \((1-r)/(1+r)\) simultaneously. The deck involution \(\tau\) sends \((b,c,d,w)\) to \((-b,-c,-d,w^{-1})\).

There is no nonzero \(\tau\)-invariant real rational phase \(b f(c,d)+B(b,c,d)w+C(b,c,d)w^{-1}\) with \(f\ne0\) and \(BC=(1+b^2)f^2/4\). Thus the exact Legendre construction with centre \(b f\) and this radius does not descend through this cover, even with rational coefficients. Indeed invariance forces \(C=\tau B\). The prime \(b^2+1\) is irreducible over the formally real circle function field \(K(c,d)\) and is fixed by \(\tau\). The valuation of \(B\tau B\) there is even, while the required radius factor has odd valuation.

## Hermitian isometry torsors detected by a Pfister neighbour

**Theorem (A Pfister witness on a point-free hermitian isometry torsor).**

Let \(D\) be a quadratic field extension of \(K\) or a quaternion division algebra over \(K\), with its canonical involution, and \(\operatorname{char}K=0\). Let \(h_1,h_2\) be nondegenerate hermitian forms of rank \(n\ge3\) over \(D\), and let \(q_i(v)=h_i(v,v)\) denote their scalar quadratic forms. In the quadratic field case fix a determinant fibre of the isometry space using a determinant value of the required norm in \(D^\times\), so the fibre is a torsor for the special unitary groups. In the quaternion case use the entire isometry torsor. Suppose that \(h_1=\langle c\rangle_D\perp h_1'\), that \(q_2\perp\langle-c\rangle\) is contained in a quadratic Pfister form \(\pi\), and that the scalar form of \(h_1'\) contains a subform similar to a subform of \(\pi\) of dimension greater than \(\frac12\dim\pi\). Then the torsor admits a regular pure phase with a fixed regular multiplier whose marked moments are nonzero infinitely often.

Use the source stabilizer \(S\) of the distinguished length-\(c\) vector. Its invariant quotient is the affine quadric \(Y=\{v:h_2(v,v)=c\}\). Geometrically in the quadratic case the vector is a pair consisting of a vector and a covector with nonzero pairing, and a determinant-one map can take any such pair to another by extending on the complement. In the quaternion case after splitting, a vector is a symplectic two-frame of nonzero pairing; extending symplectic bases proves the same quotient and shows that the fibres are \(S\)-torsors. These descriptions descend by base change of affine invariants. The function field \(F=K(Y)\) makes \(\pi\) hyperbolic. Hence the scalar form of \(h_1'\) is isotropic by its neighbour. If \(D_F\) splits, the source stabilizer is already a split special linear or symplectic group. Otherwise an isotropic vector in the scalar form is an isotropic hermitian vector, and the resulting hermitian hyperbolic plane gives a split one-parameter torus by reciprocal scaling of its two basis vectors. This has determinant one in the quadratic case. The split rank-one group obtained in \(S_F\) acts nontrivially on the generic fibre, and the generic-orbit theorem lifts its witness. For the scalar norm forms in these two cases, see [Hu, *Hasse Principle for Simply Connected Groups over Function Fields of Surfaces*, §§2.3–2.4](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1203.1075).

In the quadratic field case, use the preceding eleven-dimensional example for the diagonal coefficients of the hermitian forms and extend its ground field by an independent variable \(v\). Take \(D=K(\sqrt{-v})\), with \(v\) positive in the chosen ordering. The scalar forms are the previous quadratic forms tensored with \(\langle1,v\rangle\). Their determinant ratio as hermitian forms is a square, supplying the required determinant fibre. The Pfister criterion holds with \(\pi\) also tensored with \(\langle1,v\rangle\), and the neighbour now has dimension \(18>16\).

For a quaternion example of rank six take an independent positive-variable field \(K=\mathbb Q(u_1,u_2,u_3,v,w)\) and \(D=(-v,-w)\). Let \(\pi_0=\bigotimes_{i=1}^3\langle1,u_i\rangle\). Take \(h_2\) diagonal with any six distinct nonidentity monomials from \(\pi_0\), and \(h_1=\langle-1\rangle_D\perp h_0\), with \(h_0\) formed from any five distinct nonidentity monomials of the same form. Use \(\pi=\pi_0\otimes\langle1,v\rangle\otimes\langle1,w\rangle\). The scalar \(q_2\perp\langle1\rangle\) is contained in \(\pi\), and the scalar form of \(h_0\) has dimension \(20>16\). In both examples the distinct coefficient valuations modulo twice the value group persist for the scalar forms after tensoring, proving anisotropy over \(K\). Both torsors are point-free because in the positive ordering \(h_2\) cannot represent \(-1\) while \(h_1\) does.


## A global obstruction at a transverse zero of the imaginary radius

**Theorem (The rightmost real contact obstructs purity).**

Let \(M\) be a smooth compact manifold of dimension two or three with a positive smooth probability density. Let \(A,B\) be real smooth functions and set \(P=A+B(t-t^{-1})/2\). Suppose \(B\not\equiv0\). If \(B\) has no zeros, then \(P\) is not pure. Otherwise put \(c=\max_{\{B=0\}}A\). Suppose that at every point of \(\{A=c,B=0\}\) the function \(B\) is transverse to zero and \(A\) restricted to that hypersurface has a nondegenerate maximum. Then \(P\) is not pure for the integrated Laurent trace. Here \(c\) is taken on the zero set of \(B\) and is allowed to lie below the global maximum of \(A\). In particular the test applies to both full exceptional compact quotients and allows nonzero left-circle weights in both coefficients.

If \(P\) were pure, the Cauchy formula for the scalar circle, followed by integration, would give
\[
               I(z)=\int_M\big((z-A)^2+B^2\big)^{-1/2}\,d\mu=1/z
\]
for large positive real \(z\), using the positive square root. The positive integral is real analytic on the real interval \(z>c\). Thus the identity extends there until the first pole of \(1/z\). If \(B\) has no zeros, or if \(c<0\), analyticity at zero contradicts that pole.

If \(c\ge0\), put \(\delta=z-c\) and take local coordinates \(u,v\) at one of the finitely many maximal contacts, with \(u=B\) and
\[
                     A(u,v)=c-|v|^2+u\,a(u,v).
\]
Here the Morse lemma is used on the zero hypersurface, and \(\dim v=\dim M-1\). Put \(s=\delta+|v|^2\). With a cutoff equal to one near the contact, the integral in the normal direction has the form
\[
               J(s,v)=\int
                  \frac{\phi(u,v)\,du}{\sqrt{(s-u a(u,v))^2+u^2}},
                     \qquad \phi(0,v)>0
\]
near \(v=0\). The estimates near \(s=0^+\), uniform for small \(v\), are
\[
              J(s,v)=O(1+|\log s|),\qquad
              \partial_s J(s,v)=
              -\frac{2\phi(0,v)}{\sqrt{1+a(0,v)^2}\,s}
                          +O(1+|\log s|).
\]
For the derivative, replace \(a(u,v)\) and \(\phi(u,v)\) by their values at \(u=0\) on a small fixed interval in \(u\). Since \((s-u a)^2+u^2\) is comparable to \(s^2+u^2\) for bounded \(a\), the derivative errors are bounded by constants times \(|u|/(s^2+u^2)\) and \(u^2/(s^2+u^2)^{3/2}\). Both integrals are \(O(1+|\log s|)\). The frozen normal integral is explicit by completing the square, and differentiation yields the displayed \(1/s\) term. The first estimate also follows directly from comparability.

The error integrates to a bounded quantity in \(v\) for both tangent dimensions one and two. The first estimate therefore makes \(I(z)\) bounded as \(z\downarrow c\), excluding \(c=0\). If \(c>0\), the negative leading terms of \(I'(z)\) contribute a nonzero multiple in magnitude of \(\int_{|v|<\epsilon}(\delta+|v|^2)^{-1}dv\). This tends to infinity in both tangent dimensions. The contacts have the same sign in this calculation, and the derivative away from their neighbourhoods is bounded. This contradicts the derivative of \(1/z\) at \(c>0\).


## Function-field levels of the residual compact rank-one twists

**Theorem (Exact levels of the dihedral and small polyhedral twists).**

Let \(K\) be real closed, \(C=\mathrm{SL}_1((-1,-1)_K)\), and \(Y\) one of the point-free forms in the residual compact rank-one list. Then the level \(s(K(Y))\), the least number of squares representing \(-1\), is as follows.

| Geometric stabilizer and descent class | Level |
| --- | --- |
| Trivial, giving the negative quaternion norm torsor | \(4\) |
| Cyclic, nontrivial toral class | \(2\) for even order, \(4\) for odd order |
| Maximal torus, Weyl class | \(2\) |
| Binary dihedral of order \(4m\), \(m\ge3\) | \(2\) for odd \(m\), \(4\) for even \(m\) |
| \(Q_8\) or \(2T\), nontrivial normalizer class | \(4\) |

The even binary dihedral and \(Q_8\) forms are birational to \(S_4^-\). The \(2T\) form has a point over \(K(S_4^-)\). Among finite products of these residual forms and pointed compact homogeneous factors, the function field has level two if a level-two residual factor occurs, and level four if there is at least one residual factor and every such factor has level four.

The norm and toral cyclic calculations above already give the first three rows. In the maximal-torus row the quotient is \(S_3^-\), so its function field has level at most two by Pfister's level theorem. Geometric integrality keeps \(i=\sqrt{-1}\) out of all the fields in the table.

Let \(H_m=\langle\mu_{2m},j\rangle\) and \(\alpha\in\mu_{4m}\) be primitive. For odd \(m\), \(\alpha^m\) is a representative of \(\alpha H_m\) and normalizes \(\langle j\rangle\), taking \(j\) to \(-j\). Its square is \(-1\). Relative to the compact torus centralizing \(j\), this is a Weyl representative. Thus the compatible inclusions give a finite equivariant map of degree \(m\) from the Weyl twist of \(C/\langle j\rangle\) onto the dihedral twist. The source maps to the negative adjoint sphere by the quotient by its commuting split torus. Its generic fibre is a split one-dimensional torus torsor, which is rational by Hilbert 90. The source field consequently has level two. Springer's odd-degree theorem applied to \(\langle1,1,1\rangle\) gives the assertion for the target. See Springer, *Sur les formes quadratiques d’indice zéro*, C. R. Acad. Sci. Paris **234** (1952), 1517–1519.

For even \(m\), first work on the toral cyclic cover with stabilizer \(\mu_{2m}\). Use coordinates \(\begin{pmatrix}u&-v'\\ v&u'\end{pmatrix}\), with \(uu'+vv'=1\), and put
\[
   r=v/u,\qquad s=v'/u',\qquad A=uu'=(1+rs)^{-1},
   \qquad t=u^{2m}/A^m .
\]
These give the field \(K(i)(r,s,t)\), with \(A\) determined by \(r,s\). The twisted real involution interchanges \(r,s\) and sends \(t\) to \(-t^{-1}\) since \(\alpha^{2m}=-1\). Right multiplication by \(j\) sends
\[
   (r,s,A,t)\longmapsto(-1/s,-1/r,rsA,(s/r)^m t^{-1}).
\]
Set \(t_0=(r/s)^{m/2}t\). Twisted conjugation sends \(t_0\) to \(-t_0^{-1}\), and right \(j\) sends \(t_0\) to \(t_0^{-1}\). The real \(r,s\) field is the field of \(S_3^+\), on which right \(j\) acts antipodally. For example its three sphere coordinates are \((r+s)/(1+rs),(r-s)/(i(1+rs)),(1-rs)/(1+rs)\). In the remaining factor put
\[
                  U=(t_0-t_0^{-1})/2,\qquad V=(t_0+t_0^{-1})/(2i).
\]
These are real and satisfy \(U^2+V^2=-1\), with right \(j\) changing the sign of \(U\). If \(x\) is the sphere vector in \(S_3^+\), the invariant field is generated by \(V,Ux_1,Ux_2,Ux_3\). Indeed adjoining \(U\), whose square is \(\sum (Ux_j)^2\), recovers all four original coordinates and gives the quadratic extension. The four invariants satisfy \(V^2+\sum(Ux_j)^2=-1\), and the degree-two extension shows that they have transcendence degree three. This identifies the dihedral twist's function field with \(K(S_4^-)\).

For \(Q_8\), the adjoint description of the compact reference quotient is the space of ordered triples of pairwise orthogonal, unoriented axes. A quarter-turn interchanges the first two axes and fixes the third. Assign the eigenvalues \(i,-i,0\) to the three axes. Complex conjugation interchanges the first two eigenvalues, so the descended model is the affine variety of self-adjoint \(3\times3\) matrices for the sum of three squares with characteristic polynomial \(\lambda(\lambda^2+1)\). Every geometric matrix in this locus has distinct eigenvalues and an orthogonal eigenbasis, so this is precisely the homogeneous orbit with the required descent.

Write its generic kernel line as \(K(x,y,1)\). An orthogonal frame of the complement is \((1,0,-x),(-xy,1+x^2,-y)\). Put \(N=1+x^2+y^2\). The two squared lengths are \(1+x^2\) and \((1+x^2)N\), respectively. The complementary block, being self-adjoint and trace zero, takes the form
\[
                   \begin{pmatrix}a&Nb\\ b&-a\end{pmatrix},
                   \qquad a^2+Nb^2=-1.
\]
The map \((a,b,x,y)\mapsto(a,b,bx,by)\) to \(S_4^-\) has rational inverse where \(b\ne0\). The \(2T\) form is a finite quotient of degree three of this \(Q_8\) form, with the same quarter-turn for descent, and Springer's theorem gives the same level. The morphism of twists also sends the \(K(S_4^-)\)-point to a point on the \(2T\) form.

For a product containing a level-two factor, inclusion of that factor's field gives the upper bound two, and geometric integrality gives equality. In the remaining case every factor has a point over \(F_4=K(S_4^-)\). This gives a smooth \(F_4\)-point on the product, so its function field embeds in an iterated Laurent series field over \(F_4\): use the local ring at that point after base change, its power series completion, and then the field of iterated Laurent series. The form \(\langle1,1,1\rangle\) is anisotropic over \(F_4\) by Hoffmann's separation theorem and stays anisotropic in an iterated Laurent series field by taking lowest terms. Thus the product's field has level at least four, and inclusion of a residual factor's field gives equality.

**Theorem (Regular witnesses on products with the residual twists).**

Let \(Y\) be as in the level table, or a product as in that theorem with a residual factor. Let \(Z\) be an affine geometrically homogeneous space for a connected compact reductive group over the same real closed field. If \(s(K(Y))=2\), the normalized trace on \(Z\times Y\) fails to be Mathieu–Zhao whenever a simple derived factor acts nontrivially on \(Z\). If \(s(K(Y))=4\), the same holds whenever an active compact almost simple factor has absolute rank at least two. In particular both exceptional pointed rank-one quotients have regular witnesses after taking their product with any odd binary dihedral twist.

Set \(F=K(Y)\). At level two the Hamilton algebra splits, since \(-1\) is a norm from \(F(i)\). A compact root subgroup in an active factor therefore has split rank one over \(F\). For level four consider a compact simply connected cover of an active almost simple factor of absolute rank at least two. It has a compact subgroup with a simply connected cover of type \(A_2\) or \(C_2\). Indeed the root spaces for two adjacent simple roots and their negatives generate the rank-two subgroup, stable under compact conjugation, with a single bond giving \(A_2\) and a double bond giving \(C_2\). In the triple-bond case use the long \(A_2\) root subsystem of \(G_2\). The compact simply connected group of type \(A_2\) is the special unitary group of the sum of three hermitian squares over \(K(i)\). Over \(F\) its scalar form is the sum of six squares, which is isotropic by the level bound. The resulting hermitian hyperbolic plane gives an \(F\)-split torus. For type \(C_2\) the compact simply connected group is the isometry group of the sum of two quaternionic hermitian squares. An element of quaternion norm \(-1\) exists over \(F\) because \(-1\) is a sum of four squares, and gives an isotropic vector in that hermitian plane. Its hyperbolic basis again gives an \(F\)-split torus. In both cases the isotropic semisimple subgroup supplies a split rank-one subgroup, whose image acts nontrivially by almost simplicity of the active factor. Apply the generic invariant fibre theorem to the action of the group of \(Z\) on the product. Its invariant quotient is \(Y\), and the generic fibre is \(Z_F\).

If \(s(F)=4\), a compact rank-one factor stays anisotropic over \(F\), since its Hamilton algebra stays division. This pinpoints the limitation of this split-subgroup test for a compact group whose simple factors are all of rank one.

**Corollary (Preservation of every marked order for the exceptional pairs).**

Let \(Y\) be a residual twist or product of such twists and pointed factors with \(s(K(Y))=2\). For \(Z=C/2O\) or \(C/2I\) in its pointed compact form, \(Z\times Y\) has a regular pure phase with a fixed multiplier for which every nonzero marked order of the respective split binary-form pair survives.

Over \(F=K(Y)\), split the Hamilton algebra and transport the standard exceptional pair \(P,Q\). The equivariant identification over \(F\) descends because the geometric stabilizer is self-normalizing, as in the construction over the quaternion norm torsor. Its conditional pure moments are zero and its conditional marked moments \(c_m\) are the split constants in \(K\). Let \(\tau\) be the product of the commuting involutions that realizes the twist, using the identity on pointed factors. It is an involution of \(Y\) over \(K\). Choose a common denominator \(L\in K[Y]\setminus\{0\}\) for the transported pair. Then \(h=(L\,\tau^* L)^2\) is still a common denominator, so \(hP,hQ\) are regular. On the compact reference form, conjugation of the transported \(L\) equals the transported \(\tau^*L\). Their product is real there. Thus \(h^{m+1}\) is the square of a nonzero real regular function on a pointed compact homogeneous space, and its normalized trace is positive. Normalized trace is unchanged by the geometric identification of the two forms. The marked moment of \(hP,hQ\) on \(Z\times Y\) is consequently
\[
                          c_m\,\Lambda_Y(h^{m+1}),
\]
and its pure moments are zero. This proof uses the explicit denominator and positive squares in place of arithmetic specialization.

## Two obstructions to reflection-invariant exceptional phases

**Theorem (Quadratic and semidefinite recombinations of the reflected pair).**

In the setting of (R3), a nonzero symmetric homogeneous quadratic in \(F,F^\iota\) has a nonzero pure moment. The same conclusion holds for every nonzero polynomial of the form
\[
                 \Phi(F,F^\iota)=\pm\sum_{j=1}^d p_j(F)p_j(F^\iota)
                    \qquad(p_j\in\mathbb R[X]).
\]
In particular real symmetric coefficient matrices that are positive or negative semidefinite cannot give a pure phase by this descent.

For the quadratic write \(\Phi(X,Y)=a(X^2+Y^2)+bXY\). Let \(r\) be the first positive index with \(g_r=R_0(f^r)\ne0\), as in (R3). If \(b\ne0\), the \(r\)-th moment is the coefficient of \(X^rY^r\) in \(\Phi^r\) times \(\lambda(g_r^2)\). All other terms have one positive exponent below \(r\) or are pure end terms. The coefficient is
\[
           \sum_{l=0}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}
                \frac{r!}{l!\,l!\,(r-2l)!}\,a^{2l}b^{r-2l}.
\]
Every nonzero summand has the sign of \(b^r\), and the \(l=0\) term is nonzero. Compact square positivity proves the assertion. If \(b=0\) and \(a\ne0\), apply the linear obstruction following (R3) to the nonzero pure phase \(f^2\).

For the semidefinite assertion it suffices to take the plus sign. By bilinearity, (R3) applies to any pair of polynomials in \(F\) and \(F^\iota\). Expanding the \(m\)-th power yields a sum over ordered \(m\)-tuples, with each term equal to
\[
                  \lambda\!\left(R_0\!\left(\prod_{\nu=1}^m p_{j_\nu}(f)\right)^2\right).
\]
The rotation averages are real on the compact reference orbit by the same conjugation argument as in (R3), so all summands are nonnegative. Choose a nonzero \(p_j(f)\). Its restriction to a rotation orbit through a compact reference point where it is nonzero has opposite exponents in pairs, because \(p_j\) is real. The one-variable Laurent theorem gives some \(m\ge1\) with nonzero constant term on this orbit. The summand with all indices \(j\) is strictly positive for that \(m\). The negative sign only multiplies the resulting moment by \((-1)^m\).


## Cyclic twists of a quaternion norm-one group over arbitrary fields

**Theorem (The parity of a cyclic twist determines its generic quaternion splitting).**

Let \(D=(a,b)_K\) be a quaternion division algebra over a characteristic-zero field. Write \(E=K(\sqrt a)\), \(C=\mathrm{SL}_1(D)\), and \(T=\mathrm{R}^1_{E/K}\mathbb G_m\subset C\). For \(n\ge1\) let \(T[n]\) be the kernel of the \(n\)-th power map, and let \(Y_{n,c}\) be the twist of \(C/T[n]\) by the norm-one torus torsor \(N_{E/K}(z)=c\), \(c\in K^\times\), acting through the identification \(T/T[n]\simeq T\) by the \(n\)-th power map. Then \(K(Y_{n,c})\) is isomorphic as a \(K\)-field to
\[
 \begin{cases}
 K(\{N_{E/K}(z)=c\})(x_1,x_2), & n\text{ even},\\
 K(\{\operatorname{Nrd}_{D/K}(z)=c\}), & n\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}
\]
For even \(n\), \(D\) splits over \(K(Y_{n,c})\) exactly when \(c/b\in N_{E/K}(E^\times)\). For odd \(n\), \(D\) stays division over \(K(Y_{n,c})\) for every \(c\).

The base \(B=C/T\) has the conjugacy-orbit model of \(\sqrt a\) among trace-zero quaternions, cut out by the equation \(v^2=a\). The stabilizer of \(\sqrt a\) in \(C\) is \(T\), and over an algebraic closure every trace-zero element with square \(a\) is in its orbit. Thus \(B\) is an affine smooth quadric surface with a \(K\)-point, and it is \(K\)-rational by projection from a point on its projective closure. Let \(\beta\) be the \(T\)-torsor class of the generic fibre of \(C\to B\). The generic fibre of \(C/T[n]\to B\) has class \(n\beta\) because its structure group is identified with \(T\) by the \(n\)-th power map. Twisting adds the restriction of \([c]\in K^\times/N_{E/K}(E^\times)\). The norm class group over \(K(B)\) has exponent two. For even \(n\) the class is just \([c]\). For odd \(n\) it is the class for \(n=1\). The latter twisted homogeneous space is the reduced norm level in \(D\): the contracted product sends \([g,z]\) to \(gz\), since \((g,z)\) and \((g t,t^{-1}z)\) have the same image for \(t\in T\). Over an algebraic closure this map and the norm equation identify the same left \(C\)-torsor, so the map is an isomorphism over \(K\).

For even \(n\), the smooth projective closure of \(N_{E/K}(z)=c\) is the Severi–Brauer conic of \((a,c)_K\). The quadratic norm-conic Brauer kernel computed above is generated by \((a,c)_K\), and adjoining \(x_1,x_2\) preserves constant Brauer classes. Since \(D\) is division, it splits exactly when \([(a,b)_K]=[(a,c)_K]\), equivalently when \((a,c/b)_K\) is split, or \(c/b\) is a norm. For odd \(n\), write \(q_D\) for the anisotropic four-dimensional reduced norm form. The projective closure of the norm level has form \(q_D\perp\langle-c\rangle\). If it is isotropic, its point makes the quadric rational, so \(q_D\) stays anisotropic. Otherwise the dimensions \(4<5\) give Hoffmann's separation inequality with \(2^2=4\). Again \(q_D\) stays anisotropic, which keeps \(D\) division.

Consequently for any affine geometrically homogeneous \(C\)-space \(Z\) with a nontrivial action, the product \(Z\times Y_{n,c}\) has a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample whenever \(n\) is even and \(c/b\) is a norm. This applies to both exceptional stabilizers, also over fields where the space \(Z\) has no \(K\)-point. It follows by applying the generic invariant fibre theorem to the first factor. The splitting criterion is exact for this action of \(C\) on the generic fibre.

## Descent of the compact twist models from the rationals

**Theorem (Ground-field levels and regular exceptional pairs for the rational twist models).**

The twists in the real closed level table are defined over \(\mathbb Q\) using the rational Hamilton algebra. For any characteristic-zero field \(K\), let \(Y_K\) denote the base change of one of these models and let \(d\in\{2,4\}\) be its table entry. Then
\[
                        s(K(Y_K))=\min(s(K),d)
\]
where \(s(K)=\infty\) for a formally real field. This formula also holds with \(d\) the minimum table entry for a product of these models, with any additional smooth geometrically integral factors that have \(K\)-points. When \(d=2\), the product with either exceptional homogeneous space for the Hamilton norm-one group has a regular pure phase and a fixed regular multiplier retaining every nonzero marked order of the standard split pair.

Here are rational definitions of the descent data. The rotations are torsion group schemes of the norm-one torus of \(\mathbb Q(i)\), and \(j\) is the second quaternion generator with square \(-1\). The toral involution on a cyclic quotient is the class of an element with \(n\)-th power \(-1\); this class is rational in \(T/T[n]\), even when no representative is rational. The dihedral involution comes from the same class for \(n=2m\): it normalizes the dihedral stabilizer since a representative has square in \(T[2m]\). For \(Q_8\) and \(2T\) use a quarter-turn given in the adjoint group by conjugation by \(1+ij\). This is rational and normalizes both groups: it permutes the quaternion coordinate axes up to sign, and preserves the Hurwitz units defining \(2T\). The adjoint quotient is available since the center is in both stabilizers. The involution for the maximal torus is right \(j\), and for the trivial stabilizer it is central multiplication by \(-1\). Twist each reference quotient over \(\mathbb Q(i)/\mathbb Q\) by its indicated rational involution. These constructions commute with base change.

If \(i\in K\), the equality of levels is immediate. Otherwise the function field computations above use only the indicated involutions, the quaternion norm equation, and the quadratic extension \(K(i)/K\). In particular an even dihedral cover has the same formulas over \(K(i)\), as its twisting class acts on \(u^{2m}\) by \(-1\). For odd \(m\) the compatible Weyl cover still has degree \(m\), and its quotient by the commuting split torus still has the negative ternary norm equation. For \(Q_8\) the self-adjoint matrix model and its birational map are defined over \(K\); the degree-three morphism to the \(2T\) twist is defined there too. The cyclic twist calculation also follows from the parity theorem with \(a=b=c=-1\) when the Hamilton algebra is division. Alternatively its coordinate proof works over \(K(i)\) without that hypothesis. These give the upper bounds. The level-two case has equality by geometric integrality and \(i\notin K\). For a level-four row with \(s(K)\ge4\), the field \(F_4=K(S_4^-)\) has level four. If \(s(K)>4\), apply separation to the anisotropic forms \(\langle1,1,1\rangle\) and \(\langle1,1,1,1,1\rangle\). If \(s(K)=4\), the negative sphere has a \(K\)-point and \(F_4\) is rational over \(K\). All the level-four models have points over \(F_4\), by the birational calculations, the morphism to the \(2T\) twist, or the displayed norm equation. The same Laurent-series argument at a smooth \(F_4\)-point on their product proves the lower bound for every such product.

To construct the marked pair for \(d=2\), first work over \(\mathbb Q\) and keep the factor whose table entry is two. The split exceptional homogeneous space is defined over \(\mathbb Q\) by the standard binary form. Twisting it by the left translations of the inner cocycle of the Hamilton algebra defines the corresponding homogeneous space over \(\mathbb Q\). The equivariant isomorphism class over any \(K\) is unique: the geometric stabilizer is self-normalizing, so over an algebraic closure there is a unique equivariant identification with any other form for the same acting group, and it descends. The Hamilton algebra splits over the rational twist's function field by the level formula with \(K=\mathbb Q\). Transport the binary-form pair over that field, take a common rational denominator \(L\), and multiply both phase and multiplier by \((L\,\tau^*L)^2\) as above. Its trace factors are nonzero rational numbers because after extending to \(\mathbb R\) they are strictly positive on the compact reference form. Hence the witness and every originally nonzero marked order descend to \(\mathbb Q\) and extend to \(K\). Pullback along projection retains the moments on products with more factors.


## The reflection identity with a polynomial height weight

**Theorem (Fourier coefficients of the height-weighted reflection pairing).**

In the setting of (R3), let \(u\) be the coordinate fixed by the reflection of \(Y^-\). Write \(R_\alpha\) for rotation by \(\alpha\) in the split real rotation torus, and let \(\Pi_k g\) be the \(k\)-th Fourier component of \(g(R_\alpha x)\), taken with \(e^{-ik\alpha}\). For real polynomials \(H,p,q\), set
\[
    \widehat H_k=\int_0^{2\pi} H(i\cos\alpha)e^{-ik\alpha}
                                  \,\frac{d\alpha}{2\pi}.
\]
Then
\[
      \Lambda\bigl(H(u)p(F)q(F^\iota)\bigr)
            =\sum_{k\in2\mathbb Z}\widehat H_k\,
                    \lambda\bigl(\Pi_{-k}(p(f))\Pi_k(q(f))\bigr).
\]
For \(p=q\) this is the sum over \(k\in2\mathbb Z\) of the \(k\)-th Fourier coefficient of \(H(i\sin\alpha)\) times \(\|\Pi_k(p(f))\|_{L^2}^2\), with the norm on the compact reference orbit. Consequently no nonzero phase
\[
              \Phi=\pm\sum_j H_j(u)\,p_j(F)p_j(F^\iota)
\]
is pure if each \(H_j\) has nonnegative real coefficients as a polynomial in \(1+u^2\).

Indeed, on the cycle used in (R3), the class of the relative left translation is \(R_\alpha\) with \(\alpha=\pi/2-\theta\). The fixed coordinate is \(u=i\sin\theta=i\cos\alpha\). Conditional trace on the quotient gives \(\lambda(p(f)\,q(f)(R_\alpha x))\). Invariance under simultaneous left rotation retains exactly the components with total weight zero. Since the geometric stabilizer contains \(-I\), only even weights occur. The Fourier coefficients of \(H(i\cos\alpha)\) are symmetric under \(k\mapsto-k\), giving the displayed identity. In particular a monomial \(H(u)=u^d\) has zero contribution for odd \(d\); for even \(d\) the weights in the sum are \(i^d2^{-d}\binom d{(d+k)/2}\), taken as zero outside \(|k|\le d\).

On the compact reference orbit the conjugate of \(p(f)\) is its left translate by \(J_0\), the rotation by \(\pi/2\). Conjugating its \(k\)-th component therefore gives \((-1)^{k/2}\Pi_{-k}(p(f))\) for even \(k\). Multiplying the coefficient \(\widehat H_k\) by \((-1)^{k/2}\) shifts the angle by \(\pi/2\) and gives the coefficient of \(H(i\sin\alpha)\). This proves the positive-norm form of the identity. For the last assertion expand each power of \(\Phi\). Every product of the \(H_j(i\sin\alpha)\) has nonnegative Fourier coefficients, since \(1+(i\sin\alpha)^2=\cos^2\alpha\) does and products convolve coefficients. Each summand of the expanded moment has the same sign, or is zero. Take a \(j\) with \(H_j\ne0\) and \(p_j(f)\ne0\). The zero Fourier coefficient of \(H_j(i\sin\alpha)^m\) is positive for all \(m\), and some \(\Pi_0(p_j(f)^m)\) is nonzero by the opposite-exponent argument in (R3). The term with all indices equal to \(j\) at that \(m\) makes the moment nonzero.


## Direct denominator clearing on the exceptional reflection quotients

**Theorem (No directly cleared height phase is pure at any fixed power).**

On the octahedral quotient use \(h=p_2,\ v=9p_3/p_2\). On the icosahedral quotient use the vertex arrangement \(h=\nabla p\cdot\nabla p,\ v=36p^2/h\) from (H4). Put \(T=1+t\). For every choice of integers \(L\ge N\ge1,\ a\ge0,\ b\ge1\), the regular Laurent phase
\[
                        P=t^{-1}T^a h^L(1-T^{2b}v)^N
\]
has nonzero pure moments at infinitely many orders divisible by any given positive integer. This holds for the trace on the full exceptional compact quotient times the split scalar and over every real closed field.

The coefficients are polynomials in the invariants because \(hv\) is regular. To compute the trace use the two conditional identities and the squared-uniform law for \(v\). Write \(J_n(v)=\mathbb E[h^n\mid v]\). There are positive integers \(e,\beta\) and nonzero rational numbers \(C_n\) such that
\[
          J_n(v)=C_n\bigl(v^{en}-\beta n\,v^{en-1}+\text{lower powers}\bigr).
                                                               \tag{H17}
\]
For the octahedral case \(e=2,\ \beta=6\). In (H3), \(A=-v^2(1-6/v+O(v^{-2}))/24\), and the square root of \(B^2\) with positive leading coefficient is \(v^2(1-6/v+O(v^{-2}))/24\). Taking the conditional circle average gives (H17) with \(C_n=(-1/24)^n 2^{-n}\binom{2n}{n}\). The branch sign is irrelevant because odd powers of the cosine have zero conditional mean.

For the icosahedral case \(e=5,\ \beta=30\). Write \(-F(v,p)/(18662400p^4)=1+\sum_{j=1}^4 c_j(v)p^{-j}\), using (H5). Each \(c_j\) has degree at most three, and \(c_1=\gamma v^3(1-15/v+O(v^{-2}))\) with \(\gamma=-1/135\). In (H6), \(h^n=36^n p^{2n}v^{-n}\). At weight \(p^{-2n}\) in the inverse square root, the highest \(v\)-degree comes from \(c_1^{2n}\). Any term involving some \(c_j\) with \(j\ge2\) uses at most \(2n-1\) factors and loses at least three degrees. Thus
\[
                  C_n=36^n\binom{-1/2}{2n}\gamma^{2n}
\]
and the next coefficient is \(-30n C_n\). All conditional coefficients in both cases are integral at every prime outside a fixed finite set, before integration in \(v\). This follows in (H3) and (H6) by expanding the inverse square root, whose generalized binomial coefficients have only powers of two in their denominators, and using the polynomial divisibility in (H6).

Set \(d=2(eL+N)\), \(k=a+2bN\). After conditional averaging and extracting the scalar constant term, the resulting polynomial in \(v\) has degree at most \(dm/2\) at order \(m\). Its top two coefficients, after removing the common nonzero factor \((-1)^{Nm}C_{Lm}\), are
\[
               \binom{km}{m},\qquad
                -m\left(\beta L\binom{km}{m}
                        +N\binom{km-2b}{m}\right).               \tag{H18}
\]
Indeed the next term comes either from the second coefficient in (H17) or from choosing \(1\) in one of the \(Nm\) factors \(1-T^{2b}v\). Integrating \(v^j\) gives \(1/(2j+1)\).

Fix a desired divisor \(M\ge1\) of the orders \(m\). First take large primes \(\ell=dm+1\) with \(m\) a multiple of \(M\). There are infinitely many by Dirichlet's theorem in the progression \(1\bmod dM\). The factor \(C_{Lm}\) is an \(\ell\)-unit, since in the octahedral case \(2Lm<\ell\), and in the icosahedral case \(4Lm<\ell\), for all sufficiently large \(\ell\) under consideration. The top denominator is \(\ell\) and all lower denominators are \(\ell\)-units. Write \(k=qd+r,\ 0\le r<d\). Reduction of \((1+t)^\ell\) modulo \(\ell\) shows that \(\binom{km}{m}\) is an \(\ell\)-unit if the least base-\(\ell\) digit of \(km\) is at least \(m\). For \(r=0\) this digit is \(\ell-q\); for \(r\ge1\) it is \(rm-q\) for large \(\ell\). This proves nonvanishing unless \(r=1\) and \(q>0\).

In that case take instead primes \(\ell=dm-1\) with \(m\) a multiple of \(M\), from the progression \(-1\bmod dM\). The top two denominators are now \(\ell+2\) and \(\ell\); all other denominators are \(\ell\)-units. The least digits in (H18) become \(m+q\) and \(m+q-2b\). The first binomial is an \(\ell\)-unit. If \(q<2b\), the second is zero modulo \(\ell\). If \(q\ge2b\), the ratio of the second to the first modulo \(\ell\) equals the reduction of the fixed positive rational number
\[
                             \prod_{j=q-2b+1}^{q}\frac{j}{j+1/d}.
\]
This follows by the ratio of the binomials in the least digits and by \(m\equiv1/d\bmod\ell\). The combination in parentheses in (H18) is therefore an \(\ell\)-unit at all sufficiently large primes in this progression. Here \(m\) and \(C_{Lm}\) are again units. The penultimate coefficient supplies the unique term with negative valuation. This proves the claimed nonvanishing in every prescribed sequence of multiples. The trace moments used in the proof are rational and commute with base extension.

## A common summand in an isometry torsor and its finite quotients

**Theorem (Regular witnesses on mixed-sign classical twists with odd stabilizer).**

Let \(K\) have characteristic zero, and let the coefficient algebra \(D\) be \(K\), \(K(i)\), or the Hamilton quaternion algebra, with the conventions on split coefficient algebras as in the central classical torsor construction. Consider the isometry space from \(h_0\perp -I_r\) to \(h_0\perp I_r\), where \(I_r\) is the standard form of rank \(r\) over \(D\) and \(h_0\) is any common nondegenerate summand. For the orthogonal or special unitary model assume \(r\) even and take a fixed determinant fibre giving a torsor under the connected special isometry groups. For the quaternionic model use the whole isometry space. Let \(H\) be any finite subgroup of odd geometric order of the source group, acting by precomposition. The homogeneous quotient by \(H\), with the postcomposition action of the target group, has a regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample in each of the following cases.

- In the orthogonal and special unitary cases, \(r\) is even and is not a power of two.
- In the quaternionic case, \(r\) is not a power of two.
- In the orthogonal case, \(r=2\) and \(h_0\) contains \(I_2\) as an orthogonal summand.

In particular for the definite target form over a real closed field these cases include noncentral involutions and finite stabilizers that need not preserve a block of the defining representation.

First pass to the isometry torsor \(X\) before the finite quotient. Let \(S_0\) be the connected group of the target complement of a chosen common summand, acting trivially on that summand. The invariant quotient by \(S_0\) parametrizes its isometric embeddings into the source form, by restricting the inverse of an isometry. Over a geometric field every such embedding extends to the whole space by extending frames, and the fibres of the map are \(S_0\)-torsors. This identifies the invariant quotient by base change, since the frame space is affine and the description holds geometrically. In the quaternionic case after splitting use symplectic frames, as in the central construction. The generic embedding over the quotient field \(F_0\) has orthogonal complement isometric to the original source complement by Witt cancellation, or by extension of an isometry between the two copies of the common summand. In the orthogonal case a reflection in the complement adjusts the resulting determinant if necessary. In the unitary case scaling a diagonal line by a unit of norm one gives the required determinant adjustment. Thus the generic fibre identifies equivariantly under \(S_0\) with the isometry torsor between the two complementary forms.

For the first two cases choose the common summand \(h_0\). The generic fibre over \(F_0\) is the rank-\(r\) central torsor. Choose \(S\subset S_0\) as in the central proof, fixing one coordinate in the orthogonal, special unitary, and even quaternionic cases, and using the standard \(\mathrm{SU}(r)\) in the odd quaternionic case. It is the same embedded subgroup over \(K\) and over \(F_0\). Its scalar form becomes isotropic over \(L=K(X)^S\) by the proof for the central torsor. Here the invariants over \(F_0\) identify with \(L\) because all nonzero \(S_0\)-invariants are \(S\)-invariant and can be localized first. The central proof applies with the target group, by using the inverse isometry. This inverse exchanges the two standard complementary forms and preserves the central norm equation.

For the last case write \(h_0=h'_0\perp I_2\) and use \(h'_0\) as the common summand. The two complementary forms are the definite \(I_4\) and the split \(I_2\perp -I_2\). Their oriented exterior squares each have two Hodge eigenspaces of dimension three, since both determinants are one. In a diagonal orthogonal basis the induced forms on the two eigenspaces of \(I_4\) are proportional to \(\langle1,1,1\rangle\). For \(I_2\perp -I_2\), both are proportional to \(\langle1,-1,-1\rangle\): use the complementary pairs \(12,34\), \(13,24\), and \(14,23\), with the Hodge signs. Take \(S\subset\mathrm{SO}(I_4)\) to be the connected kernel on the plus eigenspace. Over the algebraic closure this is one of the two rank-one factors, with a central isogeny onto the special orthogonal group of the minus eigenspace. This follows from the exterior-square action on the two three-dimensional summands, which gives the usual two-factor isogeny of \(\mathrm{SO}_4\). The induced isometry between the plus eigenspaces is invariant under \(S\), so \(\langle1,1,1\rangle\) becomes isotropic over \(L\), and \(S_L\) has split rank one.

Now take \(F=K(X/H)^S=L^H\), since the actions commute. The degree of \(L/F\) divides the geometric order of \(H\): this holds after geometric scalar extension by finite invariant theory, and the degree is unchanged because the invariant quotients are geometrically integral. By Springer's theorem the relevant scalar form is isotropic over \(F\). In the unitary and quaternionic cases this gives a Hermitian hyperbolic plane if the coefficient algebra is division. If the coefficient algebra is split, the connected isometry group in question is already split. The generic invariant fibre is geometrically homogeneous under \(S_F\). Its geometric stabilizer is finite because the original quotient has finite stabilizers. Thus its split rank-one subgroup acts nontrivially, and the generic fibre obstruction gives a fixed regular witness on the quotient.

## Split actions on the residual quaternion quotients

**Theorem (Rigidity while the quaternion algebra stays division).**

Let \(C=\mathrm{SL}_1(D)\) for a quaternion algebra over a characteristic-zero field, and let \(Y\) be an affine geometrically homogeneous \(C\)-space with finite geometric stabilizer. If \(D\) stays division over \(K(Y)\), every algebraic \(\mathbb G_a\)-action and every split torus action on \(Y\) is trivial. The assertion holds even if the action does not commute with \(C\). On \(Y\times\mathbb G_m^s\), additive actions are trivial and split torus actions come from translation on the scalar factor. In particular this includes the two pointed exceptional compact products over a real closed field. It also applies to a product of level-four compact rank-one twists with finite stabilizers, as regards the projection to each such twist.

In fact let \(F/K\) be any field over which \(D_F\) is division, and let \(f\) be an \(F\)-morphism \(\mathbb A^1_F\to Y_F\) or \(\mathbb G_{m,F}\to Y_F\). Choose the point \(y=f(0)\) or \(y=f(1)\) respectively. The orbit map \(C_F\to Y_F\) at \(y\) is finite étale because the geometric stabilizer is finite in characteristic zero. Pull it back by \(f\). Over an algebraic closure, finite étale covers of \(\mathbb A^1\) are trivial and those of \(\mathbb G_m\) are trivialized by a power map. For the latter statement a connected cover extends to a cover of projective curves branched only above \(0,\infty\). Riemann–Hurwitz forces a genus-zero curve with a single point over each end, so the map is \(t\mapsto t^e\) up to coordinates. For \(\mathbb A^1\) the same formula with at most one branch value forces degree one. After the power pullback where needed, there is a unique geometric section through the identity point of \(C_F\) above the chosen parameter. This section is Galois invariant, so the resulting map lifts to \(C_F\) over \(F\). Its polynomial or Laurent polynomial coordinates satisfy the norm equation. A highest positive exponent or lowest negative exponent would have a nonzero anisotropic norm in the corresponding extreme coefficient. Thus all coordinates are constant. The original \(f\) is constant by dominance of the power map.

Apply this to the orbit of the generic point, or to each projection of that orbit for a product. A purely transcendental extension of \(K(Y)\) keeps \(D\) division, by leading coefficients. This proves that the projection to \(Y\) is fixed in a product with split scalars. An additive action on the remaining torus coordinates is constant in the additive parameter, since the units of a polynomial ring over a domain are constant in that parameter. The units of \(K[Y]\) are constants by pullback to the geometric semisimple group and descent. The units of its Laurent polynomial ring are therefore scalar monomials. For a torus action on the product, invertibility and evaluation at the identity parameter force each scalar coordinate to transform by itself times a character of the acting torus. This proves the description of split torus actions. For the exceptional pointed quotient the function field is formally real by its smooth real point, so the Hamilton algebra stays division. The level-four product case uses the previously computed product level.


## Quaternary isometry twists with an odd Hodge image

**Theorem (A regular witness from one Hodge projection).**

Let \(q_0,q_1\) be nondegenerate four-dimensional quadratic forms of square determinant over a characteristic-zero field. Assume \(q_0\) is hyperbolic. Let \(X\) be an oriented isometry torsor from \(q_0\) to \(q_1\), and let \(H\subseteq\mathrm{SO}(q_0)\) be a reductive subgroup acting by precomposition. Take one Hodge eigenspace \(W_{0,+}\subset\bigwedge^2q_0\). Suppose that the image \(H_+\) of \(H\) on \(W_{0,+}\) is finite of odd geometric order, and that \(H\) does not contain the connected kernel of this Hodge action. Then \(X/H\), acted on by \(\mathrm{SO}(q_1)\), admits a fixed regular Mathieu–Zhao counterexample.

Choose the orientations so every isometry in \(X\) respects the Hodge decompositions, and use \(W_{1,+},W_{1,-}\) for the target eigenspaces. The two eigenspace forms of either quaternary form are isometric by any orthogonal reflection, which exchanges the Hodge signs. The two forms for the hyperbolic \(q_0\) are isotropic by the diagonal calculation for \(I_2\perp -I_2\). Put \(S=\ker(\mathrm{SO}(q_1)\to\mathrm{SO}(W_{1,+}))^\circ\). Its action on \(W_{1,-}\) gives a central isogeny onto \(\mathrm{SO}(W_{1,-})\). The map from \(X\) to the induced isometry of plus eigenspaces has image a homogeneous isometry torsor \(Z\) between the two oriented ternary forms. It is surjective geometrically by the two-factor action of \(\mathrm{SO}_4\), and is equivariant for the action of \(H\) through \(H_+\). The generic isometry makes \(W_{1,+}\) isotropic over \(K(Z)\). The extension \(K(Z)/K(Z)^{H_+}\) has odd degree, so this isotropy descends by Springer. The quotient map gives an inclusion \(K(Z)^{H_+}\subseteq K(X/H)^S\). Thus \(W_{1,-}\) is isotropic over this generic invariant field, and \(S\) has split rank one there.

The dimension of \(H\) is less than three: its connected component is a proper subgroup of the connected three-dimensional kernel on \(W_{0,+}\). Consequently an \(S\)-orbit in \(X/H\) has positive dimension, and the split rank-one subgroup acts nontrivially by almost simplicity. The generic fibre theorem gives the regular witness.

For a real closed example take \(q_1=I_4\), \(q_0=I_2\perp-I_2\), and take the image of \(C_3\times C_8\) in \(\mathrm{SO}(q_0)\) under the spin cover \(\mathrm{SL}_2\times\mathrm{SL}_2\to\mathrm{SO}(q_0)\), choosing the two cyclic groups in the real rotation tori of the two factors. The first Hodge projection has order three. The subgroup has geometric order \(24\), since the diagonal central kernel intersects \(C_3\times C_8\) trivially. The quotient is point-free: a point would give a point of \(Z/H_+\) and hence an isometry of the two ternary forms over an odd-degree extension of the real closed field. That is impossible since they have different signatures. Both projections of \(H\) into the adjoint factors are nontrivial, so the connected commuting group is compact, with Lie algebra the two rotation centralizers.


## No regular algebraic reparametrization of the exceptional heights

**Theorem (Every projectable regular derivation fixes the arrangement height).**

On the full compact octahedral quotient take the height \(v\) of the coordinate mirrors or the six long-root mirrors, or take \(v=S\) from the full joint chart (H14). On the full compact icosahedral quotient take the height \(v\) of the vertex hyperplanes, the face hyperplanes, or the fifteen reflection mirrors. Let \(\delta\) be any regular derivation of the full coordinate ring over a real closed field, extended to its function field. If \(\delta(v)\) belongs to the field generated by \(v\), then \(\delta(v)=0\). The same holds after adjoining independent scalar parameters to the coefficient field. In particular a formal family of regular maps from the full quotient to itself starting at the identity cannot induce a nonconstant formal rational change of \(v\) depending on \(v\) alone.

Write \(\delta(v)=g(v)\). Work first over the complexification. Average \(\delta\) over the normalizer of the left circle defining the angular quotient, using its algebraic Reynolds projection. Derivations form a locally finite rational module: restriction to a finite-dimensional stable generating space embeds this module into the tensor product of the dual generating space and the coordinate ring. The normalizer is linearly reductive and fixes \(v\), so the averaged derivation has the same value \(g(v)\). It descends to the angular rotation quotient and commutes with the remaining inversion, which comes from the nonidentity Weyl element of the left circle. Its restriction \(\bar\delta\) to the reflection quotient is therefore a polynomial derivation in \(p,q\) or \(p,h\).

In each model let \(J\) be the orientation generator. The orientation-odd part is \(J\) times the reflection invariants, since the corresponding polynomial anti-invariants are divisible by the product of the reflecting forms. This descends to the sphere by averaging. The averaged derivation commutes with inversion, so it sends \(J\) to a multiple of \(J\). It is tangent to each reduced component of the discriminant, since \(\bar\delta(J^2)\in(J^2)\) and we work in characteristic zero in the polynomial invariant plane. In the octahedral case the discriminant is proportional to \(q f\), with \(p=p_2,\ q=p_3\) and \(f\) the discriminant of \(Z^3-Z^2+pZ-q\). In the icosahedral case it is \(f\) from (H4).

For the coordinate and vertex heights the displayed ratios \(9q/p\) and \(36p^2/h\) take every finite value on a divisor away from the polar set, and have a simple pole along \(p=0\) and \(h=0\), respectively. The other three heights have ratios
\[
          \frac{9f}{(1-3p)(p-9q)},\qquad
          \frac{100r^2}{(25+240p)r-15p(1+8p)^2},\qquad
          \frac{-225f}{80ab},
\]
in the long-root, face, and fifteen-mirror cases, respectively, with the same invariant conventions as their conditional formulas. Each has coprime numerator and denominator. For the long roots substitute \(p=1/3\) or \(q=p/9\) into \(f\). For the face height the denominator is nonzero at generic \(r=0\). For the mirror height the substitutions \(a=0\) and \(b=0\) in \(f\) give, respectively,
\[
              -\frac{(5p+1)^4(27p-1)^2}{25},
              \qquad \frac{64p^2(5p+1)^4(27p-1)^2}{(15p-1)^3}.
\]
In each ratio the numerator minus any scalar multiple of the denominator is nonconstant, and the denominator has at least one simple factor. Thus in all cases \(g\) has no finite pole. At a simple pole of \(v\) a polynomial derivation can raise its pole order by at most one, so \(g\) is a polynomial of degree at most two.

For the five arrangement heights the values \(0,1\) are critical values at regular points of \(v\) on the full compact quotient. At a generic point on a single arrangement hyperplane the numerator of the squared height has a double zero and the denominator is nonzero. This gives \(0\). In a chamber, at an interior maximum of the absolute hyperplane product the gradient is radial. Such a maximum exists since the product is nonzero in the chamber and vanishes on its boundary. The identity \(x\cdot M=D\) and the norm formula then give height \(1\). The height is regular there and its differential vanishes by the global bound \(v\le1\). These give \(g(0)=g(1)=0\) since real tangent spaces complexify to the algebraic tangent spaces. The argument applies over real closed fields by transfer, and the same critical points give the constraints after scalar extension.

For the coordinate-mirror height a third critical value comes from the smooth discriminant curve. Take \((p,q)=(-1,-1)\). The discriminant
\[
                 f=p^2-4p^3-4q-27q^2+18pq
\]
vanishes there, with gradient \((-32,32)\). Its tangent has \(dp=dq\), so \(v=9q/p\) has zero differential on it and \(v=9\). Tangency of \(\bar\delta\) to the discriminant gives \(g(9)=0\).

For the vertex height use (H5). Its discriminant as a quartic in \(p\), up to a nonzero constant, is
\[
                    v^7(v-1)^8(35v^3-810v^2-405v-2916).
\]
The cubic factor is squarefree and has no root at \(0\) or \(1\). At any root \(v_0\) of this cubic the quartic has one double root \(p_0\) and its curve is smooth there, with \(dv\) zero on the tangent. Indeed, a simple zero of the discriminant forces exactly one double root. Factoring off the other simple roots étale-locally leaves a monic quadratic whose discriminant vanishes to order one. Completing its square gives the asserted smooth ramification. Moreover \(p_0\ne0\), since \(F(v,0)=-1024v^3\). Thus the change between \(h\) and \(v\) is regular with regular inverse near that point, and \(F=0\) is the discriminant curve there by (H4)–(H5). Tangency gives \(g(v_0)=0\). For the long-root height use the component \(q=0\) of the octahedral discriminant. Setting \(q=0\) in the long-root height gives
\[
                     \frac{9p(1-4p)}{1-3p}.
\]
Its derivative vanishes at \(p=1/2\), with value \(9\) and with nonzero denominator. Tangency to \(q=0\) again gives \(g(9)=0\).

For the face height let \(\omega\) be a root of \(48\omega^2+33\omega+40\), and put \(p_0=(8\omega-7)/104\), \(r_0=\omega(25+240p_0)/200\). Substitution into \(G_w\) of the face-height fibre gives \(G_\omega=G_{\omega,p}=G_{\omega,r}=0\) and \(r_0\ne0\). Since \(G_{\omega,w}=-100r_0^2/\omega\ne0\), the height is regular and has zero differential at \((p_0,r_0)\). This gives \(g(\omega)=0\). For the fifteen-mirror height substitute \((p,r)=(11/9,-365/27)\) in \(-225f/(80ab)\). The denominator is nonzero, the value is \(25\), and both first derivatives vanish. Hence \(g(25)=0\).

For \(v=S=2(1-3p)p/(p-9q)\) in the octahedral joint chart, the same coprimality and simple-pole argument gives \(g\in K[v]\) of degree at most two. On the component \(q=0\) the height restricts to \(2-6p\), and \(\bar\delta\) preserves the intersection of \(q=0\) with \(f=0\). The latter is cut out on that line by \(p^2(1-4p)\), so the restricted derivation annihilates \(p\) at \(p=0,1/4\). Consequently \(g(2)=g(1/2)=0\). On the other component \(f=0\) use the repeated-root parameter \(x\) with \(p=x(2-3x),\ q=x^2(1-2x)\). There \(S=2-3x\), after rational cancellation. Since \(\bar\delta(p)=(2-6x)\bar\delta(x)\) lies in \(K[p(x),q(x)]\), its derivative with respect to \(x\) vanishes at \(x=1/3\), where both coordinate derivatives vanish. The product derivative at that point equals \(-6\bar\delta(x)\), and \(\bar\delta(x)=-g(2-3x)/3\) is polynomial. Thus \(g(1)=0\), which supplies the third value for the joint chart.

In all cases \(g\) vanishes at three distinct values and has degree at most two. Neither the pole argument nor these evaluations changes when independent parameters are adjoined.

For the assertion about formal maps write the formal parameter as \(\epsilon\) and let the pullback specialize to the identity at \(\epsilon=0\). If its restriction to \(v\) first differs from \(v\) at order \(r\), successively remove all lower positive jets of the pullback. At a stage where the lower jets are zero, the first remaining jet is a derivation. At an order below \(r\) it annihilates \(v\), so composition with its inverse formal exponential removes that jet and fixes \(v\).
